 \lambda\leq\rho_i\leq\Lambda\quad\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\text{-almost everywhere},
 \qquad i=0,1.$$ The almost-everywhere unique optimal map from $\rho_0\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}$ to $\rho_1\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}$ for the cost $c$ has a homeomorphic representative $\widetilde T:M\to M$, and $$d(\widetilde T(x),\widetilde T(x'))\leq C d(x,x')^\alpha,
 \qquad
 d(\widetilde T^{-1}(y),\widetilde T^{-1}(y'))
       \leq C d(y,y')^\alpha$$ for all $x,x',y,y'\in M$.*

</div>

The constants are common to the full density class on the fixed manifold. They do not depend on individual densities or their derivatives. The theorem includes conjugate cut points, and does not assume nonfocality, strict MTW, or cut avoidance. We obtain a positive exponent by contradiction; no explicit exponent or uniformity over varying metrics is asserted.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and geometric input

McCann’s polar factorization theorem supplies the almost-everywhere unique optimal map and its cost potential (McCann 2001) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[McCann2001](#ref-McCann2001)]. The curvature condition originates in the work of Ma–Trudinger–Wang (Ma, Trudinger, and Wang 2005) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[MTW2005](#ref-MTW2005)]; Loeper established its connection with supporting cost functions and continuity (Loeper 2009) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Loeper2009](#ref-Loeper2009)]. Sections and contact-volume comparison are central tools in Caffarelli’s theory (Caffarelli 1992) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Caffarelli1992](#ref-Caffarelli1992)] and in the weak-MTW regularity theory of Figalli–Kim–McCann (Figalli, Kim, and McCann 2013a, Theorem 2.1 and Corollary 9.6) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[FKM2013Holder](#ref-FKM2013Holder)]. These results already treat rough densities. Their smooth-cost Hölder estimates require the appropriate cost-convexity and nondegeneracy hypotheses. A density-controlled exponent after localization does not by itself provide a common localization scale or Hölder constant for a family whose transport graphs approach conjugate pairs.

Uniform control over a density-bounded class is known in important geometric settings. Loeper–Villani obtain such estimates under strict MTW and nonfocality, with a further extension under their uniform-regularity hypothesis and positivity of the minimizing-fiber diameter $\delta(M)$ (Loeper and Villani 2010, Corollaries 8.3–8.4) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[LoeperVillani2010](#ref-LoeperVillani2010)]. Figalli–Kim–McCann prove uniform cut separation on products of round spheres (Figalli, Kim, and McCann 2013b, Theorem 5.1 and Corollary 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[FKM2013Spheres](#ref-FKM2013Spheres)]. The issue addressed here is the general fixed weak-MTW manifold, without imposing such separation. A different quantitative stability result of Warren (Warren 2026, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Warren2026](#ref-Warren2026)] assumes common $C^2$ norm bounds on the logarithms of smooth densities and sufficiently small Wasserstein distance; those assumptions are not part of the measurable-density class considered here.

The density-free input is proved in the companion paper *Global Support and Convex Injectivity Domains under Weak MTW* (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Foundation2026](#ref-Foundation2026)]. It derives convexity of open and closed injectivity domains, global support for every ordinary subgradient, and uniform intermediate regularity from <a href="#eq:weak-mtw" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weak-mtw">[eq:weak-mtw]</a>. We state the complete interface in Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">2</a>. The global supporting conclusion has an important antecedent: Figalli–Rifford–Villani (Figalli, Rifford, and Villani 2011, Lemma 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[FRV2011](#ref-FRV2011)] prove the global double-mountain principle assuming convex injectivity domains as well as weak MTW. The companion supplies the geometric hypothesis under weak MTW alone, together with the intermediate statements needed below. Figalli–Gallouët–Rifford (Figalli, Gallouët, and Rifford 2015, Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[FGR2015](#ref-FGR2015)] previously proved the convexity implication under nonfocality. On compact surfaces, Figalli–Rifford–Villani (Figalli, Rifford, and Villani 2011, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[FRV2011](#ref-FRV2011)] proved that transport continuity is equivalent to the conjunction of convex injectivity domains and weak MTW. Their introduction poses the equivalence with weak MTW alone; the separate convexity and continuity questions also appear in (Lebedeva, Petrunin, and Zolotov 2019, Questions 8.6–8.7) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[LPZ2019](#ref-LPZ2019)].

Figalli–Rifford–Villani’s transport continuity property concerns all density pairs bounded above and away from zero. They prove that this property implies convex injectivity domains and weak MTW (Figalli, Rifford, and Villani 2011, Definition 1.1 and Theorem 1.2(i)) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[FRV2011](#ref-FRV2011)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> implies that property by choosing bounds for each pair. Combined with their necessity result, it yields the weak-MTW–transport-continuity equivalence, with the forward direction strengthened to the class-uniform estimates above.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Conventions

We use dual potentials with $$u(x)=\sup_y\{-c(x,y)-v(y)\},\qquad
 v(y)=\sup_x\{-c(x,y)-u(x)\}.$$ Thus $u(x)+c(x,y)+v(y)\geq0$, with equality at a contact. The symbol $\partial u(x)$ denotes the ordinary subdifferential of the semiconvex function, with covectors identified with tangent vectors by the metric. At a differentiable contact its gradient is the initial minimizing log. We write $$G_x=\{p\in T_xM:d(x,\exp_xp)=|p|\}=\overline{I(x)},\qquad
 c_t=c/t,\qquad Q_tf(z)=\min_x\{f(x)+c_t(x,z)\},$$ and, for $p\in I(x)$, $$A_x(p)=D^2_{xx}c(x,\exp_xp),\qquad
 \sigma_x(p)=|\det(d\exp_x)_p|.$$ The function $\sigma_x$ extends continuously to $G_x$. At an ordinary cut point, a specified nonconjugate minimizing branch has a smooth upper cost branch; we state explicitly when such a branch is used. A star denotes the adjoint. Constants denoted by $c,C$ may change between formulas. Constants in the final comparison depend only on the fixed geometry and density bounds; constants used solely to pass limits at fixed scales may also depend on the already fixed templates.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof structure

For a dual pair $(u,v)$, the lifted gap section at $x$ and height $r\geq0$ is $$\mathcal S_x(r)=\left\{p\in G_x:
       u(x)+\frac{|p|^2}{2}+v(\exp_xp)\leq r\right\}.$$ We study the oscillation of $v$ on its endpoint image $\exp_x\mathcal S_x(r)$. Convexity of the lifted section turns a power bound on that oscillation into a power bound on the diameter of the endpoint image. Taking the oscillation supremum over all admissible density pairs makes this a single scalar estimate for the entire class. Once it is proved, every zero-height section has one endpoint, and comparison of nearby poles gives the Hölder estimate in both transport directions.

The foundation theorem applies to all ordinary subgradients, including convex combinations of all minimizing logs at active cut endpoints. The projection $(x,p)\mapsto\exp_x(tp)$ of the full subgradient graph is a homeomorphism for $0<t<1$; only the supporting inequality is asserted at time one for an arbitrary potential. It also gives compact convex lifted gap sections and $C^{1,1}$ intermediate potentials. The minimizing point in $Q_tu(z)$, called its pole, depends Lipschitzly on $z$.

Section <a href="#sec:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:spectral">3</a> develops the quantitative estimates needed when a minimizing endpoint approaches conjugacy. A fixed joining point factors the full Jacobi matrix into nonsingular piece matrices and a positive semidefinite broken-action Hessian. Radial shortening adds a uniformly positive form. Transverse MTW, the radial identity, and a common separating logarithmic scale then compare ordered singular values along an extended affine segment.

Section <a href="#sc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:section">4</a> takes a supremum of section oscillations over the entire density class. Failure of every power bound gives two separated gap scales on which this supremum is almost constant. Two scalar modifications produce a positive comparison maximum. One modifies the value at a center endpoint; the other supplies the active endpoints whose minimizing logs have the center log as a convex combination. Varying their amplitude gives an inequality for every positive active barycentric representation, with constants fixed before the curvature of the outer modification is chosen.

Section <a href="#sec:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:jets">5</a> keeps the order of the limiting argument explicit. An exact pole-matrix identity transfers lower jets to active endpoints. The original contact-volume bound first excludes conjugacy at a selected outer endpoint and bounds its determinant. This bounds the center Hessian. Only then does the short-time minimizer have nonsingular derivative, allowing the original almost-everywhere density equation to be used at the sampled center endpoints. An oblique-projection determinant gain and the spectral comparison give the contradiction. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a> converts the resulting power bound into the common estimate for the map and its inverse.

<a id="sec:geometry"></a>
### The density-free foundation

We record the precise geometric input from (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Foundation2026](#ref-Foundation2026)]. Every statement in this section is density-free and uses only the fixed compact manifold and weak MTW. The proofs, including the treatment of conjugate cut endpoints, are in that paper.

A potential is a function $u(x)=\max_y\{-c(x,y)-w(y)\}$ with a continuous datum $w$; replacing $w$ by the cost transform of $u$ gives a dual pair. Put $D_g=\operatorname{diam}M$. The total minimizing-vector set $\{(x,p):p\in G_x\}$ is compact. For $p\in G_x$ and $y_s=\exp_x(sp)$, $0<s<1$, divided action gives <a id="geo:split-support"></a>$$\label{geo:split-support}
 c(z,\exp_xp)\leq c_s(z,y_s)+\frac{1-s}{2}|p|^2,
 \qquad c_s(x,y_s)=\frac{s}{2}|p|^2.$$ The right side is a smooth upper support near $x$.

<a id="stmt-geo-prelim"></a>
<div id="geo:prelim" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Uniform supports and active logs). *There is a geometric constant $C_g$ such that the cost is uniformly semiconcave in either variable, including at cut points. Every potential is $D_g$-Lipschitz and uniformly semiconvex. For a continuous representing datum $w$, define $$\mathcal V_u(x)=\{p\in G_x:
              u(x)+c(x,\exp_xp)+w(\exp_xp)=0\}.$$ Then $\mathcal V_u(x)$ is compact and $\partial u(x)=\operatorname{conv}\mathcal V_u(x)$. Every $p\in\partial u(x)$ has a positive representation using at most $n+1$ affinely independent active logs. The set $\mathcal V_u(x)$ includes every minimizing log to each endpoint active for the specified datum $w$.*

</div>

This is (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Foundation2026](#ref-Foundation2026)]. In particular, the smooth upper supports obtained by splitting at time $1/2$ have a common neighborhood size and Hessian bound. The representing datum need not itself be a cost potential.

Write $H_s(p)=A_x(sp)/s$. At a specified nonconjugate minimizing branch, $A_x$ will also denote the smooth branch Hessian.

<a id="stmt-geo-radial-index"></a>
<div id="geo:radial-index" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Radial divided action). *Let $p\in G_x$, and let $S_p(r)$ be the Jacobi tensor along $\exp_x(rp)$ with $S_p(0)=0$, $D_rS_p(0)=\mathrm{Id}$, in parallel orthonormal frames. For $0<t<s<1$, $$\frac{d}{dr}H_r(p)=-S_p(r)^{-1}S_p(r)^{-T},
 \qquad H_t(p)-H_s(p)\geq\kappa_g(s-t)\mathrm{Id},$$ where $\kappa_g>0$ depends only on the manifold. On every smooth minimizing branch, $A_x(w)w=w$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-geo-slack"></a>
<div id="geo:slack" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Transverse slack). *Let $p_i\in G_x$, $m_i>0$, $\sum_i m_i=1$, and set $p=\sum_i m_ip_i$, $E=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{p_i-p\}$. If $t\operatorname{conv}\{p_i\}\subset I(x)$ and $0<t<s<1$, then $$\left[\frac{A_x(tp)}t-\sum_i m_i\frac{A_x(sp_i)}s\right]
       \bigg|_{E^\perp}\geq\kappa_g(s-t)\mathrm{Id}.$$ The conclusion also holds at a limiting time $t$ if the radially contracted hulls lie in $I(x)$ and the displayed Hessians have nonconjugate branch limits. No minimizing assumption about $sp$ is needed in this limiting assertion.*

</div>

These are (OpenAI 2026, Lemmas 4.2–4.3) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Foundation2026](#ref-Foundation2026)]. The latter applies to every positive finite representation, not merely a selected Carathéodory representation. We reconstruct the Jacobi identities and the additional uniform spectral bounds in Section <a href="#sec:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:spectral">3</a>.

<a id="stmt-geo-short-time"></a>
<div id="geo:short-time" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Short time for a semiconvex datum). *Let $f$ be Lipschitz and semiconvex. For sufficiently small $t>0$, depending only on these two bounds and the manifold, $$F_t:\Gamma_f\to M,\qquad
 \Gamma_f=\{(x,p):p\in\partial f(x)\},\qquad
 F_t(x,p)=\exp_x(tp)$$ is a homeomorphism with locally Lipschitz inverse into the tangent bundle. The minimizing pole $x_t(z)$ of $Q_tf$ is unique, $Q_tf\in C^{1,1}$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}Q_tf(z)=-t^{-1}\log_zx_t(z)$. The graph $\Gamma_f$ is an $n$-dimensional Lipschitz manifold.*

</div>

This is (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Foundation2026](#ref-Foundation2026)]. The local mechanism is the curved version of proximal regularization and quadratic inf-convolution; compare (Moreau 1965, secs. 3–5 and 7) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Moreau1965](#ref-Moreau1965)]. It is needed below for increasing scalar modifications of potentials, which need not themselves be cost potentials.

<a id="stmt-geo-support"></a>
<div id="geo:support" class="theorem">

**Theorem 6** (Global supporting property). *For every potential $u$, every $x\in M$, and every $p\in\partial u(x)$, one has $p\in G_x$ and $$u(x')\geq u(x)+c(x,\exp_xp)-c(x',\exp_xp)
 \qquad(x'\in M).$$ For each $0<t<1$, $F_t:\Gamma_u\to M$ is a homeomorphism, $tp\in I(x)$, and its poles are the unique global minimizers of $Q_tu$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-geo-convexity"></a>
<div id="geo:convexity" class="corollary">

**Corollary 7** (Convex injectivity domains). *Both $G_x$ and $I(x)$ are convex for every $x\in M$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-geo-sections"></a>
<div id="geo:sections" class="corollary">

**Corollary 8** (Convex lifted gap sections). *For a dual pair $(u,v)$, each set $$\mathcal S_x(r)=\left\{p\in G_x:
      u(x)+\frac{|p|^2}{2}+v(\exp_xp)\leq r\right\}$$ is compact and convex, including ordinary and conjugate cut endpoints. If it is nonempty and $r\geq0$, then $$\operatorname{diam}\mathcal S_x(r)^2
 \leq8\left(r+\mathop{\mathrm{osc}}_{p\in\mathcal S_x(r)}v(\exp_xp)\right).$$*

</div>

These are (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 4.8 and Corollaries 4.9–4.10) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Foundation2026](#ref-Foundation2026)]. Convexity of injectivity domains alone is not the whole input: the ordinary-subgradient supporting assertion is also used for modified envelopes and local lower tests.

<a id="stmt-geo-intermediate"></a>
<div id="geo:intermediate" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9** (Intermediate regularity and pole control). *Let $(u,v)$ be a dual pair and $0<t<1$. Then $Q_tu=-Q_{1-t}v\in C^{1,1}(M)$, with almost-everywhere bounds $$-\frac{C_g}{1-t}g\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}Q_tu\leq\frac{C_g}{t}g.$$ If $(x_t(z),p_t(z))=F_t^{-1}(z)$ and $\Phi_s$ is geodesic flow on $TM$, then $$(x_t(z),p_t(z))=\Phi_{-t}(z,\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}Q_tu(z)).$$ In particular, the pole and momentum maps are Lipschitz with constants depending only on $M,g,t$. There are also $r_t,b_t>0$, depending only on $M,g,t$, such that every $z=\exp_x(tp)$, $p\in\partial u(x)$, satisfies $$u(x')+c_t(x',z)\geq
 u(x)+c_t(x,z)+b_t d(x,x')^2
 \qquad\text{if }d(x,x')<r_t.$$ All these constants are uniform when $t$ ranges in a compact subinterval of $(0,1)$.*

</div>

This is (OpenAI 2026, Proposition 4.11) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Foundation2026](#ref-Foundation2026)]. Its proof uses the two-sided supports from the split contact geodesic and smooth backward geodesic flow, not inversion of the full endpoint exponential map.

<a id="stmt-geo-time-one"></a>
<div id="geo:time-one" class="remark">

*Remark 10*. No time-one injectivity is claimed for an arbitrary potential. For example, $u(x)=-c(x,y_0)$ can have many poles with endpoint $y_0$. All inverse projections and $C^{1,1}$ regularizations in this paper are used at times strictly between zero and one.

</div>

<a id="sec:spectral"></a>
### Jacobi fields and uniform comparison of exponential Jacobians

This section proves two estimates that remain uniform near conjugate endpoints: a positive divided-Hessian difference after pullback by an exponential differential, and a comparison of exponential Jacobians along an extended affine segment. The radial estimates use only the fixed compact Riemannian manifold. Weak MTW enters when we compare different rays on the segment. None of these constants depends on the scalar perturbations introduced in Section <a href="#sc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:section">4</a>. The pullback estimate is Lemma <a href="#spec:pullback" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:pullback">14</a>; it supplies the positive term in the center lower bound of Lemma <a href="#jet:semibound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:semibound">29</a>. The affine-segment estimate is Lemma <a href="#spec:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:comparison">15</a>. Corollary <a href="#spec:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:outward">16</a> applies it to a positive barycentric representation. A fixed positive vertex weight supplies the opposite extension required for the affine comparison; radial and norm bounds control its remaining hypotheses. This is the geometric comparison needed in the final determinant argument.

For a linear map between tangent spaces, singular values and adjoints are defined using the Riemannian inner products. We write $$s_1(x,v)\le\cdots\le s_n(x,v)$$ for the singular values of $(d\exp_x)_v$, including zero singular values when $v\in G_x$ is conjugate. Thus $$\sigma_x(v)=\prod_{j=1}^n s_j(x,v).$$ For a positive definite symmetric matrix $Q$, the notation $\lambda_j^{\downarrow}(Q)$ lists its eigenvalues in decreasing order. All matrix identities below are written in parallel orthonormal frames; their statements are independent of that choice.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The divided-action identities

On a minimizing ray, the divided Hessian $S(T)$ below is $H_T(v)$ from Lemma <a href="#geo:radial-index" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:radial-index">3</a>. We record its Jacobi form together with reversal, and distinguish the local action from the global cost when a branch passes through cut.

<a id="stmt-spec-identities"></a>
<div id="spec:identities" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:exp-jacobi"></a>
<a id="spec:divided-hessian"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Jacobi matrices and reversal). *Let $\gamma(s)=\exp_x(sv)$ be a geodesic and identify its tangent spaces by parallel transport. Put $\mathcal R(s)w=R(w,\dot\gamma(s))\dot\gamma(s)$ and let $C,J$ solve $$U''+\mathcal R U=0,
  \qquad C(0)=\mathrm{Id},\quad C'(0)=0,\quad
  J(0)=0,\quad J'(0)=\mathrm{Id}.$$ Then $$\label{spec:exp-jacobi}
  (d\exp_x)_{Tv}=\frac{J(T)}{T}$$ for $T>0$. Whenever the geodesic to $\gamma(T)$ is nonconjugate, let $c_{\gamma,T}$ be its smooth local action: one half the squared length of the geodesic branch obtained by varying the two endpoints. The source Hessian of its divided action is $$\label{spec:divided-hessian}
  S(T):=D^2_{xx}\frac{c_{\gamma,T}(x,\gamma(T))}{T}
       =J(T)^{-1}C(T),
  \qquad
  S'(T)=-J(T)^{-1}J(T)^{-*}.$$ The endpoint is held fixed when taking $D^2_{xx}$; the derivative in $T$ then follows the displayed geodesic. The identities apply to a smooth action branch whether or not it minimizes. Before cut, this action is the cost $c$; at a nonconjugate minimizing cut vector, it is the chosen smooth upper branch of $c$.*

<a id="spec:reversal"></a>
<a id="spec:radial-identity"></a>
*If $y=\exp_xv$ and $\bar v=-P_{x\to y}v$ is the reverse initial velocity, then $$\label{spec:reversal}
  (d\exp_y)_{\bar v}=\bigl((d\exp_x)_v\bigr)^*,
  \qquad \sigma_y(\bar v)=\sigma_x(v),$$ with the indicated source and target tangent spaces. Finally, for $v\in I(x)$, $$\label{spec:radial-identity}
  A_x(v)v=v.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-identities</summary>

<a id="spec:wronskians"></a>
*Proof.* Varying the initial velocity in the geodesic equation gives <a href="#spec:exp-jacobi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:exp-jacobi">[spec:exp-jacobi]</a>. Since $\mathcal R$ is symmetric, the fundamental matrix $$\Phi(T)=
  \begin{pmatrix}C(T)&J(T)\\ C'(T)&J'(T)\end{pmatrix}$$ is symplectic. In particular, $$\label{spec:wronskians}
  J^*J'=J'^*J,
  \qquad C^*J'-C'^*J=\mathrm{Id},
  \qquad CJ^*=JC^*.$$ These identities can also be obtained by differentiating their left sides and using the Jacobi equation; their values at zero give the displayed constants.

For a variation of the initial point by $a$ with terminal variation zero, the variational field is $C(s)a+J(s)b$, where $b=-J(T)^{-1}C(T)a$. The initial gradient of the divided action is the negative initial velocity. Differentiating it in a normal frame at $x$ therefore gives $S(T)=J(T)^{-1}C(T)$. The last identity in <a href="#spec:wronskians" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:wronskians">[spec:wronskians]</a> shows that this matrix is symmetric. Moreover, the first two identities give $$J^*\bigl(C'-J'J^{-1}C\bigr)
    =J^*C'-J'^*C=-\mathrm{Id}.$$ Consequently $$\frac{d}{dT}(J^{-1}C)
    =J^{-1}\bigl(C'-J'J^{-1}C\bigr)
    =-J^{-1}J^{-*},$$ including the order of the two inverse factors in <a href="#spec:divided-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:divided-hessian">[spec:divided-hessian]</a>.

For completeness, symplecticity also gives $$\Phi(T)^{-1}=
  \begin{pmatrix}J'(T)^*&-J(T)^*\\-C'(T)^*&C(T)^*\end{pmatrix}.$$ A Jacobi field for the reversed geodesic with initial position zero and initial derivative $a$ has, in the forward parametrization, terminal data $(0,-a)$. Multiplication by this inverse matrix shows that its value at the other endpoint is $J(T)^*a$. The reversed Jacobi matrix at time $T$ is thus $J(T)^*$. Dividing both matrices by $T$ as in <a href="#spec:exp-jacobi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:exp-jacobi">[spec:exp-jacobi]</a> proves <a href="#spec:reversal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:reversal">[spec:reversal]</a>; this argument does not require $J(T)$ to be invertible.

In the radial direction the Jacobi equation has the explicit solutions $J(s)v=sv$ and $C(s)v=v$. Thus $S(T)v=v/T$. For $v\in I(x)$, taking $T=1$ gives <a href="#spec:radial-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:radial-identity">[spec:radial-identity]</a>; the case $v=0$ is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Radial shortening at a conjugate endpoint

<a id="stmt-spec-radial"></a>
<div id="spec:radial" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:singular-shortening"></a>
<a id="spec:bounded-background"></a>
<a id="spec:hessian-spectrum"></a>
<a id="spec:bounded-N"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Uniform radial transversality). *There are constants $c_{\rm r}>0$ and $C_{\rm r}<\infty$, depending only on $(M,g)$, such that for $x\in M$, $v\in G_x$, $0<\delta\le1/2$, and $1\le j\le n$, $$\label{spec:singular-shortening}
  c_{\rm r}\bigl(s_j(x,v)+\delta\bigr)
   \le s_j(x,(1-\delta)v)
   \le C_{\rm r}\bigl(s_j(x,v)+\delta\bigr).$$ There is also a positive definite form $Q_{x,v,\delta}$ on $T_xM$ such that, on putting $$N_{x,\delta}(v)
       =-\delta\bigl(A_x((1-\delta)v)-\mathrm{Id}\bigr),$$ one has $$\begin{aligned}
  &\|N_{x,\delta}(v)-Q_{x,v,\delta}\|\le C_{\rm r}\delta,
       \label{spec:bounded-background}\\
  &c_{\rm r}\frac{\delta}{s_j(x,v)+\delta}
     \le\lambda_j^{\downarrow}(Q_{x,v,\delta})
     \le C_{\rm r}\frac{\delta}{s_j(x,v)+\delta}.
       \label{spec:hessian-spectrum}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, uniformly on this set, $$\label{spec:bounded-N}
  \|N_{x,\delta}(v)\|\le C_{\rm r},\qquad
  N_{x,\delta}(v)\succeq-C_{\rm r}\delta\mathrm{Id},
  \qquad N_{x,\delta}(v)v=0.$$ No condition on the multiplicity of conjugacy is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-radial"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-radial</summary>

*Proof.* The bundle $$\mathcal G=\{(x,v):x\in M,\ v\in G_x\}$$ is compact: its defining distance equality is closed, and $|v|$ is bounded by $\operatorname{diam}(M)$. For each such vector consider $\gamma(s)=\exp_x(sv)$ on $[0,1]$. Fix the splitting time $h=1/4$ and allow the final time $T$ to vary in $[1/2,1]$. The prefix $hv$ belongs to $I(x)$, since it extends by the factor $1/h=4$ to the original minimizing ray. For the suffix, reverse the ray at $y_T=\gamma(T)$ and put $$w_T=-(T-h)\dot\gamma(T),\qquad
 a_T=\frac{T}{T-h}\ge\frac43.$$ Then $\exp_{y_T}w_T=\gamma(h)$ and $d(y_T,\exp_{y_T}(a_Tw_T))=a_T|w_T|$, so $w_T\in I(y_T)$. The reversed suffix is therefore before cut; reversal preserves uniqueness and, by Lemma <a href="#spec:identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:identities">11</a>, nonconjugacy. The parameter family is compact and its image lies in the open injectivity domain. Thus both piece differentials and their inverses have uniform bounds. Their time lengths are bounded below by $1/4$.

<a id="spec:piece-bounds"></a>
Let $C,J$ be the matrices from Lemma <a href="#spec:identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:identities">11</a>. Starting at time $h$, let $C_h(s),J_h(s)$ have the same initial data and solve the Jacobi equation along $\gamma(h+s)$. Smooth dependence on $(x,v,T)$ and the preceding nonconjugacy show that $$\label{spec:piece-bounds}
  J(h),\ J(h)^{-1},\ J_h(T-h),\ J_h(T-h)^{-1},\ C(h),\ J'(h)$$ all have uniformly bounded norms. This compactness assertion also includes $v=0$, for which $J(s)=s\mathrm{Id}$.

<a id="spec:middle-Hessian"></a>
Write $z=\gamma(h)$ and $y_T=\gamma(T)$. The Hessian in the joining point of the broken action $$z'\longmapsto \frac{c(x,z')}{h}
                     +\frac{c(z',y_T)}{T-h}$$ at $z$ is $$\label{spec:middle-Hessian}
  D(T)=J'(h)J(h)^{-1}
            +J_h(T-h)^{-1}C_h(T-h).$$ The first term is the terminal Hessian of the first piece; the second is the source Hessian of the second piece. At $T=1$, the broken action has a minimum at $z$. To see this directly, the triangle inequality and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\frac{d(x,z')^2}{2h}+\frac{d(z',y_1)^2}{2(1-h)}
    \ge\frac{(d(x,z')+d(z',y_1))^2}{2}
    \ge\frac{d(x,y_1)^2}{2},$$ with equality at $z$. Hence $D(1)\succeq0$.

<a id="spec:middle-crossing"></a>
Only the second term in <a href="#spec:middle-Hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:middle-Hessian">[spec:middle-Hessian]</a> depends on $T$. Applying <a href="#spec:divided-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:divided-hessian">[spec:divided-hessian]</a> with initial point $z$ yields $$\label{spec:middle-crossing}
\begin{split}
  D(1-\delta)&=D(1)+\delta G_\delta,\\
  G_\delta&=\frac1\delta\int_{1-\delta}^{1}
       J_h(s-h)^{-1}J_h(s-h)^{-*}\,ds,
       \qquad c\mathrm{Id}\preceq G_\delta\preceq C\mathrm{Id}.
\end{split}$$ The bounds on $G_\delta$ are precisely the bounds in <a href="#spec:piece-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:piece-bounds">[spec:piece-bounds]</a>: the smallest eigenvalue of the integrand is $\|J_h(s-h)\|^{-2}$, and its largest eigenvalue is $\|J_h(s-h)^{-1}\|^2$. Thus the crossing is positive on the whole space, including any null space of $D(1)$.

<a id="spec:Jacobi-factorization"></a>
Jacobi propagation through the joining point gives the exact factorization $$\label{spec:Jacobi-factorization}
\begin{split}
  J(T)
    &=C_h(T-h)J(h)+J_h(T-h)J'(h)\\
    &=J_h(T-h)D(T)J(h).
\end{split}$$ This formula is also valid at $T=1$ when either of the two full matrices is singular. The splitting point is kept fixed while $T$ varies; differentiating a moving midpoint is unnecessary. Taking $h=1/2$ gives the usual midpoint factorization on $T\in[3/4,1]$. The choice $h=1/4$ covers all the parameters in the statement with one set of bounds.

Write $\lambda_1(D)\le\cdots\le\lambda_n(D)$ for the increasing eigenvalues of a positive semidefinite matrix $D$. The min–max characterization applied to <a href="#spec:middle-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:middle-crossing">[spec:middle-crossing]</a> gives $$\lambda_j(D(1))+c\delta
     \le\lambda_j(D(1-\delta))
     \le\lambda_j(D(1))+C\delta.$$ Multiplication of a matrix on either side by a uniformly bounded invertible matrix changes each ordered singular value by at most a uniform multiplicative factor. Apply this fact to <a href="#spec:Jacobi-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:Jacobi-factorization">[spec:Jacobi-factorization]</a>, use $(d\exp_x)_{Tv}=J(T)/T$, and note $1/2\le T\le1$. At $T=1$, the singular values of $J(1)$ are comparable to the eigenvalues of $D(1)$; at $T=1-\delta$, they are comparable to the eigenvalues of $D(1-\delta)$. These observations prove <a href="#spec:singular-shortening" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:singular-shortening">[spec:singular-shortening]</a> with uniform constants.

<a id="spec:Schur-action"></a>
We give the Hessian calculation as well, because it identifies the bounded term that must not be discarded at a radial eigenvector. Set $$P=J(h)^{-1}C(h),\qquad E=J(h)^{-*}.$$ The source–join mixed Hessian of the first divided action is $-J(h)^{-1}=-E^*$. For $T<1$, eliminating the joining-point variation in the Hessian of the broken action gives $$\label{spec:Schur-action}
  \frac{A_x(Tv)}{T}=P-E^*D(T)^{-1}E.$$ To verify the elimination, write the quadratic form for source variation $a$ and joining variation $b$ as $a^*Pa-2a^*E^*b+b^*D(T)b$. Its unique stationary value in $b$ is at $b=D(T)^{-1}Ea$ and has value $a^*(P-E^*D(T)^{-1}E)a$. This is the Hessian of the stationary composed action. Positivity of $D(T)$ follows from <a href="#spec:middle-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:middle-crossing">[spec:middle-crossing]</a>; the composed action is the smooth cost branch of the shortened minimizing ray.

For $T=1-\delta$, formula <a href="#spec:Schur-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:Schur-action">[spec:Schur-action]</a> gives <a id="spec:Q-definition"></a>$$\label{spec:Q-definition}
\begin{split}
  N_{x,\delta}(v)&=Q_{x,v,\delta}
                         +\delta(\mathrm{Id}-TP),\\
  Q_{x,v,\delta}&=T\delta E^*D(T)^{-1}E.
\end{split}$$ The second form is positive definite. The bounds on $P,E,E^{-1}$, the min–max inequalities just proved, and inversion of $D(T)$ give <a href="#spec:bounded-background" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:bounded-background">[spec:bounded-background]</a> and <a href="#spec:hessian-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:hessian-spectrum">[spec:hessian-spectrum]</a>. They imply the norm and lower bounds in <a href="#spec:bounded-N" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:bounded-N">[spec:bounded-N]</a>. The remaining identity follows from <a href="#spec:radial-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:radial-identity">[spec:radial-identity]</a>. In particular, for $v\ne0$ the radial eigenvalue of $N_{x,\delta}(v)$ is exactly zero, while the radial singular value of $(d\exp_x)_v$ is one. These two facts are consistent because the comparison with $Q_{x,v,\delta}$ includes the bounded background $\delta(\mathrm{Id}-TP)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-radial-determinants"></a>
<div id="spec:radial-determinants" class="corollary">

<a id="spec:radial-det"></a>
<a id="spec:center-true-det"></a>
<a id="spec:outer-true-det"></a>
**Corollary 13** (Radial comparison of exponential determinants). *With a constant depending only on $(M,g)$, if $v\in G_x$ and $1/2\le\theta\le1$, then $$\label{spec:radial-det}
  \sigma_x(\theta v)\ge c\sigma_x(v).$$ Suppose $y=\exp_xp$, $p\in G_x$, and $\bar p=-P_{x\to y}p$. If $l\in[1/2,1)$ and $\bar p/l\in G_y$, then $$\label{spec:center-true-det}
  \sigma_y(\bar p/l)\le C\sigma_x(p).$$ If $l_+\in[1,2]$, then $$\label{spec:outer-true-det}
  \sigma_y(\bar p/l_+)\ge c\sigma_x(p).$$ Comparison <a href="#spec:center-true-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:center-true-det">[spec:center-true-det]</a> permits the longer reversed vector $\bar p/l$ to have a conjugate endpoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-radial-determinants"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-radial-determinants</summary>

*Proof.* For $\theta<1$, multiply the lower bounds in <a href="#spec:singular-shortening" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:singular-shortening">[spec:singular-shortening]</a> with $\delta=1-\theta$ and discard the nonnegative terms $\delta$. The case $\theta=1$ is immediate. For <a href="#spec:center-true-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:center-true-det">[spec:center-true-det]</a>, apply <a href="#spec:radial-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:radial-det">[spec:radial-det]</a> to the longer vector $\bar p/l$ with shortening factor $l$, and then use <a href="#spec:reversal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:reversal">[spec:reversal]</a>. For <a href="#spec:outer-true-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:outer-true-det">[spec:outer-true-det]</a>, apply it to $\bar p$ with shortening factor $1/l_+$ and use the same reversal identity. Thus the direction of the two inequalities reflects, respectively, a longer and a shorter true reversed ray. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### A uniform gain from an extra minimizing extension

<a id="stmt-spec-pullback"></a>
<div id="spec:pullback" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:half-extension-gain"></a>
<a id="spec:full-extension-gain"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Isotropic gain after pullback). *There is $c_{\rm p}>0$, depending only on $(M,g)$, with the following property. Let $y\in M$, $r\in T_yM$, and $1<T_*\le2$ satisfy $T_*r\in G_y$. Put $$\gamma(s)=\exp_y(sr),\quad x=\gamma(1),\quad
  p=-P_{y\to x}r,\quad L=(d\exp_x)_p,
  \qquad \tau=1+\frac{T_*-1}{2}.$$ Then all costs appearing in $$\label{spec:half-extension-gain}
  L^*\left[A_y(r)-\frac1\tau A_y(\tau r)\right]L
       \succeq c_{\rm p}(T_*-1)\mathrm{Id}$$ are smooth. If the terminal ray at time $T_*$ is nonconjugate, the same inequality holds with $\tau$ replaced by $T_*$ and its chosen smooth branch. In particular, for $T_*=1/l$ this latter inequality reads $$\label{spec:full-extension-gain}
  L^*\left[A_y(r)-l A_y(r/l)\right]L
       \succeq c_{\rm p}(1-l)\mathrm{Id},
       \qquad 1/2\le l<1.$$ Without any uniformity assertion, the source Hessian of divided action decreases strictly in the positive definite order between any two nonconjugate times on a minimizing ray.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-pullback"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-pullback</summary>

<a id="spec:pullback-transpose"></a>
<a id="spec:noncommuting-ratio"></a>
*Proof.* Every time less than $T_*$ lies strictly before the cut time of this ray, so $r$ and $\tau r$ are in $I(y)$, and the reverse vector $p$ is in $I(x)$. Let $J(s)$ be the Jacobi matrix based at $y$ with unit initial derivative. By reversal, $$\label{spec:pullback-transpose}
  L=J(1)^*.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#spec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:radial">12</a> to the minimizing vector $T_*r$, with shortening factor $1/T_*$. Since $\delta=(T_*-1)/T_*\ge(T_*-1)/2$, its lower bound gives <a id="spec:J-one-inverse"></a>$$\label{spec:J-one-inverse}
  s_1(J(1))=s_1(y,r)\ge c(T_*-1),
  \qquad \|J(1)^{-1}\|\le\frac{C}{T_*-1}.$$ There is also a uniform bound on $\|J'(s)\|$ for $0\le s\le T_*$. Indeed, $|T_*r|\le\operatorname{diam}(M)$, $T_*\le2$, and smooth Jacobi evolution on this compact set of initial data bounds its fundamental matrices. Therefore, for $1\le s\le\tau$, $$\label{spec:noncommuting-ratio}
\begin{split}
  \|J(s)J(1)^{-1}\|
   &\le 1+\|J(s)-J(1)\|\,\|J(1)^{-1}\|\\
   &\le 1+C\frac{s-1}{T_*-1}\le C.
\end{split}$$

Write $S(s)=A_y(sr)/s$. Lemma <a href="#spec:identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:identities">11</a> and <a href="#spec:pullback-transpose" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:pullback-transpose">[spec:pullback-transpose]</a> give <a id="spec:noncommuting-positive"></a>$$\label{spec:noncommuting-positive}
  L^*[-S'(s)]L
    =J(1)J(s)^{-1}J(s)^{-*}J(1)^*.$$ The matrix on the right is $B_sB_s^*$ with $B_s=J(1)J(s)^{-1}$. Its smallest eigenvalue equals $\|B_s^{-1}\|^{-2}=\|J(s)J(1)^{-1}\|^{-2}$ and is thus bounded below by a uniform positive constant by <a href="#spec:noncommuting-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:noncommuting-ratio">[spec:noncommuting-ratio]</a>. This uses the displayed order of the factors and does not commute any Jacobi matrices. Integration over $[1,\tau]$, whose length is $(T_*-1)/2$, proves <a href="#spec:half-extension-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:half-extension-gain">[spec:half-extension-gain]</a>.

If the terminal branch is nonconjugate, $S$ is defined through time $T_*$ and $-S'(s)$ is positive definite on the remaining interval. Adding its integral over $[\tau,T_*]$ preserves the lower bound. Since $T_*-1=(1-l)/l\ge1-l$, this gives <a href="#spec:full-extension-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:full-extension-gain">[spec:full-extension-gain]</a>. For arbitrary two nonconjugate times on a minimizing ray, integration of $-S'(s)=J(s)^{-1}J(s)^{-*}\succ0$ proves strict decrease. When the true terminal point is conjugate, only the half-extension formula is used; no undefined terminal Hessian has been differentiated. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### From transverse MTW to comparison along an affine segment

<a id="stmt-spec-comparison"></a>
<div id="spec:comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:segment-hypotheses"></a>
<a id="spec:ordered-comparison"></a>
<a id="spec:product-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Uniform comparison along an extended segment). *Assume weak MTW. Fix $a_0>0$ and $B<\infty$. There is $c_{\rm s}>0$, depending only on $(M,g),a_0,B$, such that the following holds. Let $x\in M$, $p,q\in T_xM$, $q\ne0$, satisfy $$\label{spec:segment-hypotheses}
  p+tq\in G_x\quad(-a_0\le t\le1),\qquad
  p\cdot q>0,\qquad
  \frac{|q|^2}{p\cdot q}\le B.$$ Then, for every $1\le j\le n$, $$\label{spec:ordered-comparison}
  s_j(x,p)\ge c_{\rm s}s_j(x,p+q).$$ In particular, $$\label{spec:product-comparison}
  \sigma_x(p)\ge c_{\rm s}^n\sigma_x(p+q).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-comparison</summary>

<a id="spec:L-bounds"></a>
<a id="spec:F-definition"></a>
*Proof.* If there is a configuration in the statement then $B>0$. Set $$a=\min\{a_0,(2B)^{-1}\}.$$ We use only the segment $[-a,1]$. First suppose that all its points lie in $I(x)$. Write $$p(t)=p+tq,\qquad \beta=\frac{|q|^2}{p\cdot q},\qquad
  L(t)=\mathrm{Id}+t\frac{qq^*}{p\cdot q}.$$ The eigenvalues of $L(t)$ are $1$ on $q^\perp$ and $1+t\beta$ on $\mathbb Rq$. Hence $$\label{spec:L-bounds}
  \|L(t)\|\le1+B,\qquad \|L(t)^{-1}\|\le2
       \qquad(-a\le t\le1).$$ For $0<\delta\le1/2$, put $$\label{spec:F-definition}
  N_\delta(t)=-\delta\bigl(A_x((1-\delta)p(t))-\mathrm{Id}\bigr),
  \qquad F_\delta(t)=L(t)^*N_\delta(t)L(t).$$ These matrices are uniformly bounded by Lemma <a href="#spec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:radial">12</a> and <a href="#spec:L-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:L-bounds">[spec:L-bounds]</a>, and $F_\delta(t)\succeq-C\delta\mathrm{Id}$.

<a id="spec:transverse-conjugation"></a>
We next prove full matrix convexity of $F_\delta$. For any fixed $z\in T_xM$, the vector $$\xi=z-\frac{z\cdot q}{p\cdot q}p$$ is perpendicular to $q$. Moreover, $$\xi-L(t)z=-\frac{z\cdot q}{p\cdot q}p(t).$$ The last vector belongs to the kernel of $N_\delta(t)$ by the radial identity. Symmetry consequently gives the exact equality $$\label{spec:transverse-conjugation}
  z^*F_\delta(t)z=\xi^*N_\delta(t)\xi.$$ Weak MTW means that $t\mapsto A_x((1-\delta)p(t))(\xi,\xi)$ is concave whenever $\xi\perp q$. The negative sign in the definition of $N_\delta$ therefore makes the right side of <a href="#spec:transverse-conjugation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:transverse-conjugation">[spec:transverse-conjugation]</a> convex. Since this holds for every $z$, $F_\delta$ is convex in the Loewner order.

This argument remains uniform when $p\cdot q$ is small. The fixed test vector $\xi$ itself might be large, but its value modulo the radial kernel is $L(t)z$. Equations <a href="#spec:L-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:L-bounds">[spec:L-bounds]</a> and <a href="#spec:transverse-conjugation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:transverse-conjugation">[spec:transverse-conjugation]</a> bound the scalar convex function by $C|z|^2$, independently of the size of $\xi$. For any compact interval $K\subset(-a,1)$, a real convex function bounded in absolute value by $C|z|^2$ has slopes on $K$ bounded by $2C|z|^2/\operatorname{dist}(K,\{-a,1\})$: sandwich each secant slope between secants to the two endpoints. Applying this estimate to basis vectors and their sums and differences shows by polarization that every matrix entry of $F_\delta$ has a uniform Lipschitz bound on $K$.

To turn this bounded matrix convexity into a spectral comparison, we argue by contradiction. The spectral profile $\delta/(s_i+\delta)$ from <a href="#spec:hessian-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:hessian-spectrum">[spec:hessian-spectrum]</a> detects singular values below the radial scale: it stays positive when $s_i\ll\delta$ and tends to zero when $s_i\gg\delta$. At a scale separated from the singular values, the rank of a limiting matrix therefore counts those below the scale. A failing ordered singular-value ratio will provide a radial scale between the center and endpoint values. We choose it to separate asymptotically from every singular value at each fixed point of a countable dense set of segment parameters. The resulting positive semidefinite matrix limit has a uniform gap between zero and its positive eigenvalues, forcing constant interior rank. Convexity then prevents rank loss at the endpoint, whereas the failing ratio will force precisely such a loss.

<a id="spec:separating-scale"></a>
<a id="spec:dense-separation"></a>
Suppose that no uniform constant in <a href="#spec:ordered-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:ordered-comparison">[spec:ordered-comparison]</a> exists. There are configurations $(x_k,p_k,q_k)$ satisfying the same $a_0,B$, an index $j$ fixed after passing to a subsequence, and <a id="spec:failed-ratio"></a>$$\label{spec:failed-ratio}
  \frac{s_j(x_k,p_k)}{s_j(x_k,p_k+q_k)}\longrightarrow0.$$ For now all the segments are in their open injectivity domains, so the singular values are positive. Choose an orthonormal identification of each $T_{x_k}M$ with $\mathbb R^n$. No smooth choice in $k$ is needed. Abbreviate $$s_{i,k}(t)=s_i(x_k,p_k+tq_k),\qquad
  R_k=\log\frac{s_{j,k}(1)}{s_{j,k}(0)}\longrightarrow\infty.$$ Let $\{t_1,t_2,\ldots\}$ be a fixed countable dense subset of $(-a,1)$, enlarged to include $0$ and $1$. We now choose one radial scale that separates every fixed member of this set. Take $\log\delta_k$ in the middle half of the interval $$[\log s_{j,k}(0),\log s_{j,k}(1)].$$ Exclude the intervals of radius $R_k^{1/3}$ centered at $$\log s_{i,k}(t_m),\qquad
  1\le i\le n,\quad 1\le m\le\lfloor R_k^{1/3}\rfloor.$$ Their total length is at most $2nR_k^{2/3}$, while the available middle interval has length $R_k/2$. For all sufficiently large $k$ there is a remaining point, which we choose as $\log\delta_k$. This construction ensures $$\begin{aligned}
  &s_{j,k}(0)/\delta_k\longrightarrow0,
       \qquad s_{j,k}(1)/\delta_k\longrightarrow\infty,
       \label{spec:separating-scale}\\
  &\left|\log\frac{s_{i,k}(t_m)}{\delta_k}\right|
       \longrightarrow\infty
       \quad\text{for every fixed }i,m.
       \label{spec:dense-separation}
\end{aligned}$$ All singular values on $\mathcal G$ are uniformly bounded above. Since $\delta_k\le s_{j,k}(1)e^{-R_k/4}$, we also have $\delta_k\to0$. In particular these scales are eventually admissible in Lemma <a href="#spec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:radial">12</a>.

Form the matrices $F_k(t)$ in <a href="#spec:F-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:F-definition">[spec:F-definition]</a> using the $k$th segment and scale $\delta_k$. Their uniform bounds and interior Lipschitz bounds give, by successive extraction on compact subintervals, a locally uniform limit $F(t)$ on $(-a,1)$. Extract also a limit $E=\lim_kF_k(1)$. Both $F(t)$ and $E$ are positive semidefinite, because their approximants are bounded below by $-C\delta_k\mathrm{Id}$. The limit $F$ is matrix convex and bounded.

<a id="spec:rank-spectral-profile"></a>
To identify its possible ranks, use <a href="#spec:bounded-background" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:bounded-background">[spec:bounded-background]</a> to write $$F_k(t)=L_k(t)^*Q_{x_k,p_k+tq_k,\delta_k}L_k(t)
                     +\mathcal E_k(t),
  \qquad \|\mathcal E_k(t)\|\le C\delta_k.$$ The positive form in this expression has its $i$th decreasing eigenvalue between uniform multiples of $$\label{spec:rank-spectral-profile}
  \frac{\delta_k}{s_{i,k}(t)+\delta_k},$$ by <a href="#spec:hessian-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:hessian-spectrum">[spec:hessian-spectrum]</a> and <a href="#spec:L-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:L-bounds">[spec:L-bounds]</a>. At a fixed dense point $t_m$, put $\eta_k=\exp(-R_k^{1/3})$. For all sufficiently large $k$, depending on $m$, <a href="#spec:dense-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:dense-separation">[spec:dense-separation]</a> says that $s_{i,k}(t_m)/\delta_k$ is at most $\eta_k$ or at least $\eta_k^{-1}$. Weyl’s eigenvalue inequality therefore gives one of the two alternatives $$\lambda_i^\downarrow(F_k(t_m))
       \ge \frac{c_0}{1+\eta_k}-C\delta_k,
 \qquad\text{or}\qquad
 -C\delta_k\le\lambda_i^\downarrow(F_k(t_m))
       \le C_0\eta_k+C\delta_k,$$ with $c_0,C_0>0$ uniform in $i,m,k$. Since $F_k(t_m)$ already converges, its ordered eigenvalues converge. Their limits are therefore either zero or at least $c_0$, even if the alternatives initially alternate. No simultaneous rate at all dense points is required. Set $c_*=c_0/2$.

<a id="spec:uniform-limit-gap"></a>
The ordered eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix are continuous in its entries. Density of the set $\{t_m\}$ and continuity of $F(t)$ therefore give $$\label{spec:uniform-limit-gap}
  \operatorname{spec}F(t)\subset\{0\}\cup[c_*,C_*]
        \qquad(-a<t<1)$$ after decreasing $c_*$ once if necessary. Indeed, an eigenvalue strictly between zero and $c_*$ at an interior point would remain in that interval in a neighborhood and contradict the dense-set conclusion. The rank is now locally constant, because a continuous eigenvalue cannot pass from zero to a positive value without entering that gap. It is consequently constant on the connected interval $(-a,1)$.

<a id="spec:endpoint-order"></a>
The endpoint also has to be treated; constancy of the interior rank alone would not suffice. For every fixed vector $z$, the bounded scalar convex function $z^*F(t)z$ has a finite limit as $t\uparrow1$. Polarization yields a matrix limit $F(1-)$. The gap <a href="#spec:uniform-limit-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:uniform-limit-gap">[spec:uniform-limit-gap]</a> persists in this limit, so its rank equals the interior rank. For fixed $s<t<1$, matrix convexity of $F_k$ says $$F_k(t)\preceq\frac{1-t}{1-s}F_k(s)
                         +\frac{t-s}{1-s}F_k(1).$$ First let $k\to\infty$, then let $t\uparrow1$; this gives $$\label{spec:endpoint-order}
  F(1-)\preceq E.$$ Positive semidefinite order implies $\operatorname{rank}E\ge\operatorname{rank}F(1-)$, since the kernel of $E$ is contained in the kernel of $F(1-)$.

On the other hand, the first inequality in <a href="#spec:separating-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:separating-scale">[spec:separating-scale]</a> and the ordering of singular values make the first $j$ eigenvalues in <a href="#spec:rank-spectral-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:rank-spectral-profile">[spec:rank-spectral-profile]</a> at $t=0$ bounded away from zero. Thus $\operatorname{rank}F(0)\ge j$. The second inequality makes all eigenvalues with decreasing index $i\ge j$ tend to zero at $t=1$, so $\operatorname{rank}E\le j-1$. This contradicts the interior rank constancy and <a href="#spec:endpoint-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:endpoint-order">[spec:endpoint-order]</a>. The uniform ordered comparison is proved for open segments.

Finally, replace a closed segment in <a href="#spec:segment-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:segment-hypotheses">[spec:segment-hypotheses]</a> by $(1-\varepsilon)(p+tq)$, with $0<\varepsilon<1$. It lies in $I(x)$ by radial shortening. Its extension interval and the ratio $|q|^2/(p\cdot q)$ are unchanged, so the same constant applies. Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and using continuity of the singular values proves <a href="#spec:ordered-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:ordered-comparison">[spec:ordered-comparison]</a> on $G_x$. Multiplication over $j$ proves <a href="#spec:product-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:product-comparison">[spec:product-comparison]</a>. Every bound in the contradiction argument depended only on $(M,g),a_0,B$, which establishes the stated uniformity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Jacobian comparison for a barycentric configuration

<a id="stmt-spec-outward"></a>
<div id="spec:outward" class="corollary">

<a id="spec:outward-data"></a>
**Corollary 16** (Barycentric configurations). *Assume weak MTW and the convexity of $G_x$ established in Corollary <a href="#geo:convexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:convexity">7</a>. Fix $\mu\in(0,1)$ and positive constants $\kappa,L_0$. Suppose $$p=\sum_{i=1}^N m_i p_i,\qquad
  p_i\in G_x,\quad m_i>0,\quad\sum_i m_i=1,$$ and for one index $i$ there are $D>0$, a unit vector $e$, and $t_i>0$ such that $$\label{spec:outward-data}
  p_i=p+t_i e,\quad h:=p\cdot e>0,\quad
  h^2\ge\kappa D,\quad
  |p_i|^2-|p|^2\le L_0D,\quad m_i\ge\mu.$$ Then <a id="spec:outward-det"></a>$$\label{spec:outward-det}
  \sigma_x(p)\ge c\sigma_x(p_i),$$ where $c>0$ depends only on $(M,g),\mu,\kappa,L_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-outward"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-outward</summary>

*Proof.* Set $q=t_ie$. Expansion of the squared norms in <a href="#spec:outward-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:outward-data">[spec:outward-data]</a> gives $$2ht_i+t_i^2\le L_0D,
  \qquad
  \frac{|q|^2}{p\cdot q}=\frac{t_i}{h}
          \le\frac{L_0D}{2h^2}\le\frac{L_0}{2\kappa}.$$ Because $q\ne0$, $m_i<1$. The remaining barycenter belongs to $G_x$ by its convexity and is $$\frac1{1-m_i}\sum_{j\ne i}m_jp_j
       =p-\frac{m_i}{1-m_i}q.$$ The coefficient $m_i/(1-m_i)$ is at least $m_i\ge\mu$. Convexity therefore puts $p+s q$ in $G_x$ for every $s\in[-\mu,1]$. Lemma <a href="#spec:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:comparison">15</a> applies with $a_0=\mu$ and $B=L_0/(2\kappa)$ and proves the result. No upper bound on $m_i/(1-m_i)$ is needed: a shorter common opposite extension is enough. ◻

</details>

<a id="sc:section"></a>
### Sections, scales, and a quantitative separation

Throughout this section the manifold and the density bounds are fixed. We use the negative-cost convention $$f^c(x)=\max_{y\in M}\{-c(x,y)-f(y)\}.$$ Thus a cost-dual pair satisfies $u=v^c$, $v=u^c$, and $u(x)+c(x,y)+v(y)\geq0$. Let $\mathcal C$ denote the class of these pairs for all admissible density pairs, with any fixed additive normalization. All members of $\mathcal C$ have a common Lipschitz bound, and their oscillations are uniformly bounded. None of the arguments below uses derivatives of the densities.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A scalar quantity controlling both transport directions

For $(u,v)\in\mathcal C$ and $r\geq0$, put $$S_r(x;u,v)=\{y\in M:u(x)+c(x,y)+v(y)\leq r\},
 \qquad
 F(r)=\sup_{(u,v)\in\mathcal C,\ x\in M}
       \mathop{\mathrm{osc}}_{S_r(x;u,v)}v.$$ The sets $S_r$ are nonempty and compact. The function $F$ is bounded and nondecreasing.

<a id="stmt-sc-power"></a>
<div id="sc:power" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (The power reduction). *If there are $\beta>0$, $C_0<\infty$, and $r_0>0$ such that $F(r)\leq C_0r^\beta$ for $0<r<r_0$, then all the optimal maps in the fixed density class have homeomorphic representatives with a common Hölder estimate in both directions. One may take any exponent no larger than $\frac12\min\{\beta,1\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-power"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-power</summary>

<a id="sc:diameter"></a>
*Proof.* Write $$h_x(p)=u(x)+\frac12|p|^2+v(\exp_xp),\qquad p\in G_x.$$ By Corollary <a href="#geo:sections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:sections">8</a>, the lifted section $\{p\in G_x:h_x(p)\leq r\}$ is convex. If $p,q$ belong to it and $m=(p+q)/2$, the squared-norm identity gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac18|p-q|^2
 &=v(\exp_xm)-\frac{v(\exp_xp)+v(\exp_xq)}2
   +\frac{h_x(p)+h_x(q)}2-h_x(m) \notag\\
 &\leq F(r)+r.                                      \label{sc:diameter}
\end{aligned}$$ The exponential map has a common Lipschitz bound on the compact bundle of minimizing velocities. Hence the target diameter of a section is at most $C\sqrt{F(r)+r}$.

Let $(x,y)$ and $(x',y')$ be contacts of the same dual pair. The common Lipschitz bounds for $u$ and $c$ imply $$0\leq u(x)+c(x,y')+v(y')\leq C d(x,x').$$ Thus $y,y'$ belong to a section at $x$ of that height. For sufficiently small $d(x,x')$, <a href="#sc:diameter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:diameter">[sc:diameter]</a> gives $$d(y,y')\leq C d(x,x')^{\frac12\min\{\beta,1\}}.$$ In particular, letting the section height tend to zero shows that every pole has exactly one contact endpoint. The bound proves that this endpoint depends continuously on the pole. The class also contains the reversed pair $(v,u)$ after renormalization, which does not affect its section oscillations. The same reasoning therefore gives a unique continuous contact in the reverse direction. The dual identities give a contact above every point of either copy of $M$, and the two resulting maps are inverse. They agree almost everywhere with the optimal maps by optimality and uniqueness. Finally, compactness absorbs the remaining, larger distances into the same uniform constant. ◻

</details>

We shall contradict failure of a power bound for $F$. The following elementary selection makes the required quantifiers explicit.

<a id="stmt-sc-plateau"></a>
<div id="sc:plateau" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:endlevels"></a>
<a id="sc:plateau-data"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Logarithmic plateaus). *Suppose that $F$ has no power upper bound near zero. There are $r_j<R_j\to0$, pairs $(u_j,v_j)\in\mathcal C$, poles $x_j$, and numbers $a_j,D_j$ with $D_j>0$ such that $$\begin{gathered}
 \min_{S_{r_j}(x_j;u_j,v_j)}v_j=a_j,\qquad
 \max_{S_{r_j}(x_j;u_j,v_j)}v_j=a_j+D_j,               \label{sc:endlevels}\\
 \frac{r_j}{R_j}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 F(R_j)\leq(1+\delta_j)D_j,\qquad \delta_j\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \frac{|\log D_j|}{|\log R_j|}\longrightarrow0.       \label{sc:plateau-data}
\end{gathered}$$ Consequently $R_j/D_j^k\to0$ for every fixed $k>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-plateau"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-plateau</summary>

*Proof.* If $F$ vanished at a positive scale, monotonicity would already give a power bound below that scale. Thus $F(r)>0$. Fix $C_F\geq\max\{1,\sup_r F(r)\}$ and set $$f(s)=\log\frac{C_F}{F(e^{-s})}.$$ This function is nonnegative and nondecreasing. Failure of every power bound implies $\liminf_{s\to\infty}f(s)/s=0$; otherwise $f(s)\geq\beta s$ eventually for some $\beta>0$. Choose $N_j\to\infty$ with $f(N_j)=o(N_j)$, and divide $[N_j/2,N_j]$ into $$m_j=\left\lceil\sqrt{N_j\max\{1,f(N_j)\}}\right\rceil$$ equal intervals. Their length $N_j/(2m_j)$ tends to infinity. Since their total $f$-increment is at most $f(N_j)$, some interval $[A_j,B_j]$ has increment at most $f(N_j)/m_j=o(1)$. Put $R_j=e^{-A_j}$ and $r_j=e^{-B_j}$. Then $$\log(R_j/r_j)=B_j-A_j\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad
 \frac{F(R_j)}{F(r_j)}=e^{f(B_j)-f(A_j)}\longrightarrow1.$$ Choose an actual section with oscillation $D_j\geq(1-1/j)F(r_j)$, discarding the first few indices. Its extrema are attained, which gives <a href="#sc:endlevels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:endlevels">[sc:endlevels]</a>. Moreover $A_j\geq N_j/2$ and $f(B_j)\leq f(N_j)=o(N_j)$, so $|\log D_j|=o(A_j)$. This proves <a href="#sc:plateau-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:plateau-data">[sc:plateau-data]</a>. Finally $$\log(R_j/D_j^k)=-A_j-k\log D_j=-A_j+o(A_j)\longrightarrow-\infty.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="sc:smallness"></a>
We suppress the index $j$ from now on. All asymptotic statements refer to the sequence in Lemma <a href="#sc:plateau" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:plateau">18</a>, after the templates below have been fixed. It is not necessary that $D$ tend to zero. Set <a id="sc:binterval"></a>$$b_-=r(R/r)^{1/3},\qquad b_+=r(R/r)^{2/3}.
 \label{sc:binterval}$$ Then $r\ll b_-\ll b_+\ll R$. Uniformly for $b\in[b_-,b_+]$ and $0\leq\tau\leq8b/D$, $$\frac rb\to0,\quad \frac bR\to0,\quad
 \frac b{D^k}\to0\ (k>0\text{ fixed}),\quad
 \frac\tau D\to0,\quad \frac{\tau^2}{b}\to0.
 \label{sc:smallness}$$ For example, $\tau^2/b\leq64b/D^2$. These estimates, rather than an additional restriction on $R/r$, will control the Taylor errors.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Templates and the order of choices

At the normalized level $s=(v-a)/D$, we will use the two modifications $v+bB_c(s)$ and $v+bB_o(s)$. The first is the center datum; we compare it with the double cost transform of the second. A positive excess in this comparison will produce an interior maximum. We first fix the center profile and a cap on the outer values, then choose the outer curvature parameter $K$ and its profile, and only afterward take the gap scales small. The construction below makes that order explicit.

<a id="sc:numerical"></a>
We use the fixed numerical choices $$\varepsilon_0=2^{-10},\qquad c_0=2^{-10},\qquad c_1=c_0/4.
 \label{sc:numerical}$$ The constants are deliberately smaller than needed.

The value constraints below serve the envelope comparison: the center profile stays near one on the lower half of the selected oscillation, whereas the outer profile vanishes at level one and places a barrier on intermediate levels. We also prescribe strong outer concavity where the outer value is bounded by a fixed cap. That derivative condition will enter the endpoint lower tests in Section <a href="#sec:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:jets">5</a>; the height bound below keeps the constants in the switch and excess bounds independent of $K$.

<a id="stmt-sc-templates"></a>
<div id="sc:templates" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:barrier"></a>
<a id="sc:curvature"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Smooth templates). *There is a fixed smooth function $B_c:\mathbb R\to(-1,1)$ with bounded derivatives such that $$B_c'<0\text{ on }\mathbb R,\qquad
 B_c(s)\geq1-\varepsilon_0\quad(s\leq1/2),\qquad
 B_c(s)<0\quad(s\geq3/4).$$ Given a cap $M_0\geq2$ and $K\geq1$, there are $0<\eta\leq1/128$ and a smooth bounded function $B_o\geq0$, with bounded derivatives, satisfying <a id="sc:outer-values"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &B_o(s)\geq1\quad(s\leq1-\eta),\qquad
 B_o(1)=0,\qquad 1\leq B_o(0)\leq1+\varepsilon_0,       \label{sc:outer-values}\\
 &B_o(s)\geq B_o(0)+1
       \quad(1/16\leq s\leq1-\eta),            \label{sc:barrier}\\
 &B_o'(s)>0,\quad B_o''(s)\leq-K
       \quad\text{if }-2\eta\leq s\leq1-\eta
                      \text{ and }B_o(s)\leq M_0.     \label{sc:curvature}
\end{aligned}$$ One can arrange $\|B_o\|_\infty\leq2M_0+8$, independently of $K$, and make $\eta$ arbitrarily smaller if desired.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-templates"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-templates</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $\gamma$ so large that $2/(1+e^{\gamma/8})\leq\varepsilon_0$, and set $$B_c(s)=1-\frac{2}{1+e^{-\gamma(s-5/8)}}.$$ This has all the asserted properties. In particular, $|B_c'|$ has a positive minimum on every fixed compact interval.

For the outer function let $$\beta(z)=\begin{cases}e^{-1/z},&z>0,\\0,&z\leq0,\end{cases}
 \qquad
 \chi(z)=\frac{\beta(z)}{\beta(z)+\beta(1-z)}.$$ Thus $\chi$ is a smooth step, equal to zero on $(-\infty,0]$ and to one on $[1,\infty)$. Put $$\kappa=K+1,\qquad A=M_0+3,\qquad
 L=128A\kappa,\qquad h_0=\frac1{128\kappa},\qquad
 0<\eta\leq\min\left\{\frac1{128},\frac{\varepsilon_0}{24L}\right\}.$$ Define $$\begin{aligned}
 w(s)&=(L-\kappa s)
       \chi\!\left(\frac{s+3\eta}{\eta}\right)
       \left[1-\chi\!\left(\frac{s-h_0}{h_0}\right)\right],\\
 J(s)&=1+\frac{\varepsilon_0}2+\int_0^s w(q)\,dq,\\
 B_o(s)&=J(s)
       \left[1-\chi\!\left(\frac{s-(1-\eta)}{\eta}\right)\right].
\end{aligned}$$ The function $w$ is nonnegative and supported in $[-3\eta,2h_0]$. Also $$\int_{-3\eta}^0w\leq3\eta(L+3\kappa\eta)
       \leq6\eta L\leq\varepsilon_0/4,\qquad
 \int_0^{2h_0}w\leq2Lh_0=2A.$$ It follows that $$1+\varepsilon_0/4\leq J(s)\leq1+\varepsilon_0/2+2A.$$ On $[-2\eta,h_0]$, one has $J'=L-\kappa s>0$ and $J''=-\kappa$. Moreover $$J(h_0)-J(0)=A-\frac1{32768\kappa}>M_0+1.$$ Since $J$ is nondecreasing, the condition $B_o(s)\leq M_0$ on $[-2\eta,1-\eta]$ forces $s<h_0$; here $B_o=J$, proving <a href="#sc:curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:curvature">[sc:curvature]</a>. The function $J$ is constant beyond $2h_0\leq1/64$, so <a href="#sc:barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:barrier">[sc:barrier]</a> also follows. The final factor is identically one up to $1-\eta$, and decreases to zero only in $(1-\eta,1)$. This proves the remaining assertions, including smoothness at all joins and the uniform height bound. ◻

</details>

The cap $M_0$ will be fixed independently of $K$ by Lemma <a href="#sc:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:outward">25</a>; the choice $M_0=24(n+1)/c_1$ suffices. The center template is already fixed. After fixing $M_0$, choose $K$ and the outer template, and only then take the scales small. Besides <a href="#sc:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:smallness">[sc:smallness]</a>, this order gives <a id="sc:template-smallness"></a>$$\delta_j=o(\eta),\qquad
 \frac bD\max\{\|B_o'\|_\infty,\|B_c'\|_\infty\}\to0,\qquad
 \frac b{D^2}\max\{\|B_o''\|_\infty,\|B_c''\|_\infty\}\to0.
 \label{sc:template-smallness}$$ In particular, both scalar modifications used below are increasing. Constants in intermediate local estimates may depend on a fixed template, but the constants $c_0,c_1$ and the cap do not depend on $K$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The first switch

<a id="sc:g-bounds"></a>
Fix a selected section with extreme endpoints $y_0,y_1$, so that $v(y_0)=a$ and $v(y_1)=a+D$. Choose minimizing logs $p_0,p_1\in G_{x_0}$ to these endpoints, and put $P=|p_0|^2$. For every target write $$s_y=\frac{v(y)-a}{D},\qquad
 w_{o,b}(y)=v(y)+bB_o(s_y),\qquad
 w_{c,b}(y)=v(y)+bB_c(s_y),\qquad
 g_b=w_{o,b}^c.$$ Let $H_o=\|B_o\|_\infty$. Since $B_o\geq0$, $$u-bH_o\leq g_b\leq u.
 \label{sc:g-bounds}$$

We will move the pole toward $y_0$. Initially the outer modification lowers the level-zero mountain by about $b$, while leaving $y_1$ unpenalized. As long as mountains with $s_y\geq1-\eta$ dominate, their minimizing logs are shorter than the log to $y_0$, so they grow more slowly along this motion. The barrier excludes intermediate-level winners, forcing a switch with both a low and a high active endpoint. Their two logs will then provide a supported intermediate log with a prescribed squared norm; the next subsection converts this construction into a positive excess.

<a id="sc:cost-upper"></a>
<a id="sc:advance"></a>
We record the uniform distance estimate used in the proof. Splitting a minimizing geodesic at its midpoint gives a smooth upper support for the squared-distance cost with a uniformly bounded Hessian. Consequently, for any minimizing log $q$ from $x$ to $y$, $$c(\exp_x z,y)\leq c(x,y)-\langle q,z\rangle+C_g|z|^2
 \quad\text{for }|z|\text{ sufficiently small},
 \label{sc:cost-upper}$$ with one geometric constant and radius. Along a smooth curve, the one-sided derivative of $-c(\cdot,y)$ is the maximum of the pairings with its minimizing logs. In particular, whenever this restriction is differentiable, $$\frac d{d\tau}[-c(x_\tau,y)]
       \leq d(x_\tau,y)|\dot x_\tau|.
 \label{sc:advance}$$ These assertions hold also at a cut endpoint. The same upper derivative bound applies to an active mountain of a maximum: at a time when the maximum is differentiable, a semiconvex active mountain has the same derivative. Indeed its one-sided derivatives straddle that derivative in the opposite order to their semiconvex ordering, and hence coincide. The restrictions in question are Lipschitz, so these differential inequalities can be integrated.

<a id="stmt-sc-switch"></a>
<div id="sc:switch" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:switch-time"></a>
<a id="sc:switch-norms"></a>
**Lemma 20** (First switch with absolute level control). *For all sufficiently small selected scales, uniformly for $b\in[b_-,b_+]$, there is $$\frac b{32D}\leq\tau_b\leq\frac{8b}D
 \label{sc:switch-time}$$ such that, at $x_b=\exp_{x_0}(\tau_b p_0)$, the function $g_b$ has active endpoints $y_-$ and $y_+$ with $$-2\eta\leq s_{y_-}<1/16,\qquad s_{y_+}\geq1-\eta.$$ The original endpoints $y_0,y_1$ and every active endpoint at this pole lie in one original section $S_{C b}(x_b;u,v)$, where $C$ depends only on the geometry and $H_o$. In particular every active level is in $[-\delta_j,1+\delta_j]$. For every minimizing log $q_i$ to an active endpoint, $$|q_i|^2=P-2D s_{y_i}+O_{H_o}(\tau_b+b)
        =P-2D s_{y_i}+o(D).
 \label{sc:switch-norms}$$ The constants in <a href="#sc:switch-time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:switch-time">[sc:switch-time]</a> are independent of $K$ and of the height and derivatives of the outer template.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-switch"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-switch</summary>

<a id="sc:initial-logs"></a>
*Proof.* Set $x_\tau=\exp_{x_0}(\tau p_0)$ for $0\leq\tau\leq T:=8b/D$, and let $\Pi_\tau$ be parallel transport along this geodesic. For small scales $T<1/2$. The left cost is exact: $$f_y(\tau):=-c(x_\tau,y)-v(y),\qquad
 f_0(\tau)=-\frac12(1-\tau)^2P-a,\qquad
 q_0(\tau)=(1-\tau)\Pi_\tau p_0.$$ The initial gaps at $y_0,y_1$ lie in $[0,r]$, whence $$P-|p_1|^2=2D+O(r),\qquad
 |p_0-p_1|^2\leq8(D+r),\qquad
 P-\langle p_0,p_1\rangle\leq6D
 \label{sc:initial-logs}$$ for small scales; the second inequality is <a href="#sc:diameter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:diameter">[sc:diameter]</a> applied using the actual oscillation $D$ of this section. In particular $P\geq D$.

First exclude the middle levels. If $1/16\leq s_y\leq1-\eta$, put $$h_y(\tau)=f_y(\tau)-bB_o(s_y)
                -f_0(\tau)+bB_o(0).$$ Initially $h_y(0)\leq r-b$. Wherever $h_y\geq-b/2$, the identity relating values and squared distances gives, for every minimizing log $q_y$ at the current pole, $$|q_y|^2-|q_0|^2
   =-2Ds_y-2b(B_o(s_y)-B_o(0))-2h_y
   \leq-D/8-b.$$ Equations <a href="#sc:advance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:advance">[sc:advance]</a> and $\dot x_\tau=\Pi_\tau p_0$ imply $h_y'\leq-D/16$ at almost every such time. The positive part of $h_y+b/2$ is therefore nonincreasing and starts at zero. Thus $h_y\leq-b/2$ throughout $[0,T]$. No middle-level mountain can be active.

<a id="sc:left-anchor"></a>
Let $G_L$ and $G_R$ be the maxima of $f_y-bB_o(s_y)$ over the compact sets $s_y\leq1/16$ and $s_y\geq1-\eta$, respectively. Both are continuous, and the preceding paragraph shows $g_b(x_\tau)=\max\{G_L(\tau),G_R(\tau)\}$. Initially $G_L(0)\leq u(x_0)-b$ and $G_R(0)\geq f_1(0)\geq u(x_0)-r$. While $G_R>G_L$, every active endpoint is right and comparison with the left modified mountain gives $$|q_y|^2\leq|q_0|^2-2D(1-\eta)+2bB_o(0)
             \leq|q_0|^2-D.$$ By rationalizing the difference of the square roots in <a href="#sc:advance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:advance">[sc:advance]</a>, $$\frac d{d\tau}\{g_b(x_\tau)-f_0(\tau)\}\leq-D/2
 \quad\text{almost everywhere while }G_R>G_L.$$ This difference starts at most $r$ and is always at least $-bB_o(0)\geq-2b$. It cannot obey the displayed inequality throughout $[0,T]$. There is therefore a first equality time $\tau_b\leq T$. At that time both maxima are attained, and the middle-level exclusion gives $s_{y_-}<1/16$. Throughout the preceding interval, including its endpoint, $$-bB_o(0)\leq g_b(x_\tau)-f_0(\tau)\leq r.
 \label{sc:left-anchor}$$

We next anchor the absolute levels. By <a href="#sc:g-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:g-bounds">[sc:g-bounds]</a> and <a href="#sc:left-anchor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:left-anchor">[sc:left-anchor]</a>, $u(x_\tau)-f_0(\tau)\leq bH_o+r$. The initial-cost upper support and <a href="#sc:initial-logs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:initial-logs">[sc:initial-logs]</a> give <a id="sc:benchmark"></a>$$f_1(\tau)\geq
 f_0(0)-r+(P-6D)\tau-C_g\tau^2=:\mathcal L(\tau).
 \label{sc:benchmark}$$ Since $f_0(\tau)=f_0(0)+P\tau-P\tau^2/2$, it follows that $f_0-f_1\leq r+6D\tau+C\tau^2=O(b)$ on this interval. Thus both $y_0,y_1$ lie in an original section of height $Cb$. For any active endpoint $y$, $$u(x_\tau)+c(x_\tau,y)+v(y)
      =u(x_\tau)-g_b(x_\tau)-bB_o(s_y)\leq bH_o.$$ All these endpoints belong to the same section of height $Cb<R$. Its oscillation is at most $(1+\delta_j)D$ and it contains levels $a,a+D$. Hence every active level lies in $[-\delta_j,1+\delta_j]$, proving in particular the required lower bound for $s_{y_-}$.

To prove the lower time estimate, keep the winning left endpoint $y_-$ fixed, and put $$k(\tau)=f_{y_-}(\tau)-bB_o(s_{y_-})-\mathcal L(\tau).$$ One has $k(0)\leq2r-b<-b/2$, while $k(\tau_b)\geq0$, since $g_b(x_{\tau_b})\geq f_1(\tau_b)$. Let $\theta$ be the last time before $\tau_b$ at which $k=-b/2$. Then $k\geq-b/2$ on $[\theta,\tau_b]$. On this interval, using $s_{y_-}\geq-2\eta$ and $B_o\geq0$, the inequality defining this last strip gives $$\begin{aligned}
 |q_{y_-}|^2-|q_0|^2
 &\leq4\eta D+2r+12D\tau+2C_g\tau^2+b\\
 &\leq D
\end{aligned}$$ for all sufficiently small scales. This uses only <a href="#sc:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:smallness">[sc:smallness]</a>; in particular $\tau/D\to0$. Rationalizing once more gives $f_{y_-}'\leq(1-\tau)P+2D$ almost everywhere in the strip. Since $\mathcal L'=P-6D-2C_g\tau$, we have $k'\leq10D$ there for small scales. Therefore $$b/2\leq k(\tau_b)-k(\theta)\leq10D(\tau_b-\theta),$$ which implies the stated weaker lower bound $\tau_b\geq b/(32D)$. No upper bound for the template or its derivatives occurs in this last estimate.

Finally, if $y_i$ is active at the switch, the exact equality of its modified value with $g_b$ gives $$|q_i|^2=(1-\tau_b)^2P-2Ds_{y_i}
         -2bB_o(s_{y_i})-2\{g_b(x_b)-f_0(\tau_b)\}.$$ Equation <a href="#sc:left-anchor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:left-anchor">[sc:left-anchor]</a> and <a href="#sc:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:smallness">[sc:smallness]</a> prove <a href="#sc:switch-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:switch-norms">[sc:switch-norms]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Positive excess and an interior maximum

<a id="sc:excess-upper"></a>
Write $\widehat w_b=g_b^c=w_{o,b}^{cc}$ and $$E_b(y)=w_{c,b}(y)-\widehat w_b(y),\qquad
 E(b)=\max_{y\in M}E_b(y).$$ Order reversal of one cost transform and the double-transform inequality give $$v\leq\widehat w_b\leq w_{o,b},\qquad
 E_b(y)\leq bB_c(s_y)\leq b.
 \label{sc:excess-upper}$$

<a id="stmt-sc-excess"></a>
<div id="sc:excess" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:excess-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Uniform positive excess). *For all sufficiently small selected scales and all $b\in[b_-,b_+]$, $$c_0b\leq E(b)\leq b,
 \label{sc:excess-bounds}$$ with the constant in <a href="#sc:numerical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:numerical">[sc:numerical]</a>, independently of $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-excess"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-excess</summary>

*Proof.* Use the two active logs at the switch supplied by Lemma <a href="#sc:switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:switch">20</a>. Equation <a href="#sc:switch-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:switch-norms">[sc:switch-norms]</a> and their level bounds show that their squared norms lie on opposite sides of $P-D/2$. By continuity along their segment, choose $p\in\partial g_b(x_b)$ with $$|p|^2=P-D/2.$$ Theorem <a href="#geo:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:support">6</a> says that $p$ is minimizing and that $y=\exp_{x_b}p$ is a contact endpoint for $g_b$. Thus $$E_b(y)=v(y)+bB_c(s_y)+c(x_b,y)+g_b(x_b).$$ Minorizing $g_b$ by the left modified mountain gives the direct bound <a id="sc:direct-excess"></a>$$E_b(y)\geq D(s_y-1/4)+P\tau_b-P\tau_b^2/2
                  +b\{B_c(s_y)-B_o(0)\}.
 \label{sc:direct-excess}$$ If $s_y>1/2$, the right side is at least $D/4-3b$, which is larger than $c_0b$ for small scales.

<a id="sc:geometric-excess"></a>
It remains to consider $s_y\leq1/2$. The original support inequality at $x_0$ yields $$v(y)-a\geq c(x_0,y_0)-c(x_0,y)-r.$$ Apply <a href="#sc:cost-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:cost-upper">[sc:cost-upper]</a> at $x_b$ with backward displacement $-\tau_b\Pi_{\tau_b}p_0$ to get $$c(x_b,y)-c(x_0,y)
       \geq-\tau_b\langle p,\Pi_{\tau_b}p_0\rangle-C\tau_b^2.$$ The corresponding cost difference for $y_0$ is exactly $-P\tau_b+P\tau_b^2/2$. Therefore $$\frac{E_b(y)}b\geq B_c(s_y)-B_o(0)
   +\bigl(P-\langle p,\Pi_{\tau_b}p_0\rangle\bigr)\frac{\tau_b}b
   -\frac rb-C\frac{\tau_b^2}b.
 \label{sc:geometric-excess}$$ The norm choice gives $$P-\langle p,\Pi_{\tau_b}p_0\rangle
   =\frac{P-|p|^2+|p-\Pi_{\tau_b}p_0|^2}{2}\geq D/4.$$ The middle term in <a href="#sc:geometric-excess" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:geometric-excess">[sc:geometric-excess]</a> is consequently at least $1/128$. The template difference is at least $-2\varepsilon_0$, and the last two terms tend to zero uniformly by <a href="#sc:smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:smallness">[sc:smallness]</a>. The numerical choices <a href="#sc:numerical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:numerical">[sc:numerical]</a> give the asserted lower bound. The upper bound is <a href="#sc:excess-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:excess-upper">[sc:excess-upper]</a>. ◻

</details>

To obtain an interior maximum, we now subtract a penalty from the excess. A small linear term preserves its positive lower bound, and a quadratic term makes the upper amplitude boundary unfavorable. Define <a id="sc:penalty"></a>$$\pi(b)=\frac{c_0}4b+\frac{2b^2}{b_+}.
 \label{sc:penalty}$$ In particular, $\pi'(b)\geq c_1$.

<a id="stmt-sc-maximum"></a>
<div id="sc:maximum" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:aligned"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Regularized interior maxima). *Fix a sufficiently small selected scale. For every $t<1$ sufficiently close to one, the continuous function $$\Phi_t(b,z)=Q_tg_b(z)+Q_{1-t}w_{c,b}(z)-\pi(b),\qquad
 (b,z)\in[b_-,b_+]\times M,$$ has a positive global maximum with $b$ in the interior of its interval. Both spatial terms are $C^{1,1}$, and both minimizing points are unique. If $x$ and $y$ are these minimizing points at a maximum, there is $p\in\partial g_b(x)\cap G_x$ such that $$z=\exp_x(tp),\qquad y=\exp_xp,\qquad
 Q_tg_b(z)+Q_{1-t}w_{c,b}(z)=E_b(y)>0.
 \label{sc:aligned}$$ Moreover $(x,y)$ is before cut.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-maximum"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-maximum</summary>

*Proof.* At $b=b_+$, <a href="#sc:excess-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:excess-bounds">[sc:excess-bounds]</a> makes $E(b)-\pi(b)$ negative. At $b=b_-$, it is at most $b_-$. At $b_*=c_0b_+/8$, which belongs to the interval for small scales, $$E(b_*)-\pi(b_*)\geq c_0^2b_+/16.$$ Since $b_-/b_+\to0$, these inequalities give a strictly positive interior maximum with a positive separation from both boundary values.

For a fixed scale and $b$ in its compact interval, $Q_tg_b\to Q_1g_b=-\widehat w_b$ uniformly as $t\uparrow1$. This follows directly from the uniform bound for $|c/t-c|$. The functions $w_{c,b}$ have a common Lipschitz constant $L_*$ on that interval, and $$w_{c,b}-\tfrac12L_*^2(1-t)\leq Q_{1-t}w_{c,b}\leq w_{c,b}.$$ Thus $\Phi_t\to E_b-\pi$ uniformly in $(b,z)$, preserving the positive interior maximum when $t$ is sufficiently close to one.

Proposition <a href="#geo:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:intermediate">9</a> gives the asserted regularity and unique pole for $Q_tg_b$. To check the other term, the scalar function $q\mapsto q+bB_c((q-a)/D)$ has positive derivative, and its first two derivatives are bounded at the fixed scales. Composing a Lipschitz semiconvex function with this function preserves semiconvexity: the lower Taylor support for $v$, its Lipschitz bound, and the ordinary second-order Taylor inequality for the scalar function give a common quadratic lower support for $w_{c,b}$. For completeness, a minimizer in $Q_\epsilon w_{c,b}(z)$ has $d(z,y)\leq2L_*\epsilon$, by comparison with $y=z$. For small $\epsilon$, all minimizers therefore lie in one convex normal ball, where the Hessian of $c(z,\cdot)/\epsilon$ dominates the negative semiconvexity bound. Strict convexity gives uniqueness. In one fixed coordinate chart, let $K_*$ be the semiconvexity constant and let $\mu/\epsilon$ be a lower Hessian bound for the smooth cost term. The subgradient inequalities at the minimizers $y,y'$ for nearby $z,z'$ and the mixed cost derivative bound give $$(\mu/\epsilon-K_*)|y-y'|^2
       \leq (C/\epsilon)|z-z'|\,|y-y'|.$$ For small $\epsilon$ this proves local Lipschitz dependence on $z$. The envelope gradient formula then gives $C^{1,1}$ regularity. This is uniform in $b$ on the fixed interval.

At a maximum, the two spatial gradients sum to zero. Their envelope formulas show that the geodesic from $x$ to $z$, continued for the remaining time $1-t$ with the same velocity, reaches $y$. The pole minimum gives $p\in\partial g_b(x)$.
