By Theorem <a href="#geo:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:support">6</a>, this full vector is minimizing and its endpoint is a support for $g_b$. Splitting its energy at time $t$ proves <a href="#sc:aligned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:aligned">[sc:aligned]</a> exactly.

Finally put $l=1+bB_c'(s_y)/D\in(0,1)$. The short final minimization gives its backward velocity in $\partial w_{c,b}(y)=l\partial v(y)$; the equality follows from the increasing scalar chain rule, which follows in both directions by Taylor expansion and inversion of that scalar function. Dividing by $l$ gives a minimizing velocity by Theorem <a href="#geo:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:support">6</a>. The backward velocity to $x$ is a strict radial shortening of this vector, and is therefore before cut. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The parameter inequality for every representation

<a id="stmt-sc-parameter"></a>
<div id="sc:parameter" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:parameter-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 23** (The parameter derivative). *At any maximum in Lemma <a href="#sc:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:maximum">22</a>, let $$p=\sum_{i=1}^N m_i p_i,\qquad m_i>0,\qquad\sum_i m_i=1,$$ be any finite representation by minimizing logs active for the displayed representation $g_b=w_{o,b}^c$ at its minimizing pole $x$. Thus $g_b(x)+c(x,\exp_xp_i)+w_{o,b}(\exp_xp_i)=0$. Write $y_i=\exp_xp_i$ and $s_i=(v(y_i)-a)/D$, and let $s=(v(y)-a)/D$ be the center level. Then $$B_c(s)-\sum_i m_iB_o(s_i)\geq \pi'(b)\geq c_1.
 \label{sc:parameter-inequality}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-parameter"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-parameter</summary>

<a id="sc:pole-movement"></a>
*Proof.* Fix the prefix time and the spatial maximum point $z$, and abbreviate $A(b)=Q_tg_b(z)$ and $C(b)=Q_{1-t}w_{c,b}(z)$. Decrease $b$ by $h>0$, and let $x_h$ be the new pole for $A(b-h)$. Because $0\leq g_{b-h}-g_b\leq hH_o$, compactness and uniqueness of the old pole imply $x_h\to x$. The strict local quadratic growth in Proposition <a href="#geo:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:intermediate">9</a> gives, in normal coordinates $\Delta_h=\exp_x^{-1}x_h$, $$|\Delta_h|=O(\sqrt h),\qquad 0\leq A(b-h)-A(b)\leq hH_o.
 \label{sc:pole-movement}$$

<a id="sc:individual-pole"></a>
<a id="sc:linear-movement"></a>
Choose one duration $q\in(t,1)$ and split each active endpoint cost at $z_i=\exp_x(qp_i)$. The energy inequality $$c(x',y_i)\leq c_q(x',z_i)+c_{1-q}(z_i,y_i)$$ is an equality at $x'=x$. Its first term is smooth near $x$, because $qp_i$ is before cut. Its gradient is $-p_i$ and its Hessian is $A_x(qp_i)/q$. Combining this upper support with the smooth prefix cost gives $$\begin{aligned}
 A(b-h)&\geq A(b)+hB_o(s_i)
       +\langle p_i-p,\Delta_h\rangle
       +\frac12 Q_i(\Delta_h,\Delta_h)+o(|\Delta_h|^2), \label{sc:individual-pole}\\
 Q_i&=\frac1t A_x(tp)-\frac1q A_x(qp_i).\notag
\end{aligned}$$ All expansions here concern fixed scales, a fixed time, and a fixed finite representation. Equations <a href="#sc:pole-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:pole-movement">[sc:pole-movement]</a>–<a href="#sc:individual-pole" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:individual-pole">[sc:individual-pole]</a> bound each $\langle p_i-p,\Delta_h\rangle$ above by $O(h)$. Their positive weighted sum is zero, so each is also bounded below by $-O(h)$. Hence, for $E=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{p_i-p\}$, $$|\operatorname{proj}_E\Delta_h|=O(h).
 \label{sc:linear-movement}$$

<a id="sc:transverse-pole"></a>
On $E^\perp$, MTW concavity on the simplex of the $qp_i$ gives $$A_x(qp)\geq\sum_i m_iA_x(qp_i).$$ Radial monotonicity of divided action, equivalently Lemma <a href="#geo:slack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:slack">4</a>, then gives $$\sum_i m_iQ_i
 =\frac1t A_x(tp)-\sum_i\frac{m_i}qA_x(qp_i)\geq0
 \quad\text{on }E^\perp.
 \label{sc:transverse-pole}$$ Average <a href="#sc:individual-pole" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:individual-pole">[sc:individual-pole]</a>. The linear terms cancel. By <a href="#sc:pole-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:pole-movement">[sc:pole-movement]</a>, <a href="#sc:linear-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:linear-movement">[sc:linear-movement]</a>, and <a href="#sc:transverse-pole" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:transverse-pole">[sc:transverse-pole]</a>, its quadratic term is bounded below by $-o(h)$: the transverse part is nonnegative, mixed terms are $O(h^{3/2})$, and the remaining part is $O(h^2)$. The Taylor remainders are $o(h)$ as well. Thus $$A(b-h)-A(b)\geq h\sum_i m_iB_o(s_i)+o(h).$$ The unique center minimizer and the elementary envelope argument (compare the old and new minimizers and use their convergence) give $$C(b-h)-C(b)=-hB_c(s)+o(h).$$ Since $b$ is an interior maximum of $A+C-\pi$, division of the resulting one-sided comparison by $h$ and passage to the limit give <a href="#sc:parameter-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:parameter-inequality">[sc:parameter-inequality]</a>. The argument applies to the arbitrary representation fixed at the start, proving the asserted universal quantifier. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-sc-levels"></a>
<div id="sc:levels" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:level-identity"></a>
<a id="sc:level-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Levels at a regularized maximum). *At a maximum in Lemma <a href="#sc:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:maximum">22</a>, the center and all active endpoints belong to $S_{(H_o+2)b}(x;u,v)$. For every active log $p_i$, $$v(y_i)-v(y)=\frac{|p|^2-|p_i|^2}{2}
          +b\{B_c(s)-B_o(s_i)\}-E_b(y).
 \label{sc:level-identity}$$ In every positive barycentric representation the total weight on levels $s_i\geq1-\eta$ is at least $c_1$. Moreover $$s<3/4,\qquad
 \max\{s,s_i\}-\min\{s,s_i\}\leq1+\delta_j,\qquad
 \min\{s,s_i\}\geq-2\eta
 \label{sc:level-bounds}$$ for small scales. These conclusions hold at each exact maximum, before taking a prefix-time limit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-levels"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-levels</summary>

*Proof.* An active endpoint has original gap $u-g_b-bB_o(s_i)\in[0,bH_o]$. By <a href="#sc:aligned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:aligned">[sc:aligned]</a>, the center gap is $u-g_b+E_b(y)-bB_c(s)$, which is at most $(H_o+2)b$ by <a href="#sc:excess-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:excess-upper">[sc:excess-upper]</a>. This proves the common section assertion and its oscillation bound. Subtracting the active contact equality from <a href="#sc:aligned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:aligned">[sc:aligned]</a> gives <a href="#sc:level-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:level-identity">[sc:level-identity]</a>. Since $E_b(y)>0$ and $E_b(y)\leq bB_c(s)$, one has $B_c(s)>0$, and therefore $s<3/4$.

Let $m_R$ be the total right weight. Nonnegativity of $B_o$ and its lower bound by one outside the right region give $$1-m_R\leq\sum_i m_iB_o(s_i)\leq B_c(s)-c_1\leq1-c_1.$$ Thus $m_R\geq c_1$. In particular a right endpoint is present. The common range bound then puts every level above $(1-\eta)-(1+\delta_j)=-\eta-\delta_j\geq-2\eta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### A quantitative outward endpoint

The next Lemma selects an endpoint once the active logs lie on a line and the center log has a nonzero component along that line. The center Hessian estimate in Section <a href="#sec:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:jets">5</a> will establish these two properties; the selection itself is finite-dimensional.

<a id="stmt-sc-outward"></a>
<div id="sc:outward" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:outward-weight"></a>
<a id="sc:outward-norm"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Weight, cap, and norm increment). *Suppose $p=\sum_{i=1}^N m_ip_i$ in an $n$-dimensional Euclidean space, with $N\leq n+1$, $m_i>0$, and $\sum_i m_i=1$. Suppose $E=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{p_i-p\}=\mathbb Re$, where $|e|=1$, and $q=p\cdot e>0$. Assume the template and level inequalities <a href="#sc:parameter-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:parameter-inequality">[sc:parameter-inequality]</a>, <a href="#sc:level-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:level-bounds">[sc:level-bounds]</a>, and $$\left|\ |p_i|^2-|p|^2+2D(s_i-s)\ \right|\leq\zeta D,\qquad
 0\leq\delta_j\leq\zeta\leq1/16.$$ There is an outward index $i$ with $p_i=p+d_i e$, $d_i>0$, such that <a id="sc:outward-cap"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 m_i\geq m_*:=\frac{c_1}{12(n+1)},\qquad
 m_id_i\geq\frac{c_1D}{8(n+1)q},\qquad
 d_i\leq\frac{3D}{2q},                         \label{sc:outward-weight}\\
 0<|p_i|^2-|p|^2\leq3D,                       \label{sc:outward-norm}\\
 -2\eta\leq s_i<1-\eta,\qquad B_o(s_i)\leq1/m_*.
                                                        \label{sc:outward-cap}
\end{gathered}$$ In particular the cap $M_0=2/m_*$ can be fixed before $K$. All these constants are independent of $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-outward"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-outward</summary>

*Proof.* Write $p_i=p+d_i e$. The level inequality gives total right mass at least $c_1$, as in Lemma <a href="#sc:levels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:levels">24</a>. A right point has $s_i-s>1/4-\eta$, hence $$|p_i|^2-|p|^2=2qd_i+d_i^2\leq-D/4.$$ It follows that $d_i<0$ and $|d_i|\geq D/(8q)$. Every outward point satisfies, by the common level range, $$0<2qd_i+d_i^2\leq2D(1+\delta_j)+\zeta D\leq3D,$$ and so $d_i\leq3D/(2q)$. The barycenter identity $\sum_i m_id_i=0$ therefore implies $$\sum_{d_i>0}m_id_i\geq\frac{c_1D}{8q}.$$ Choose an outward index contributing at least $1/N$ of this moment. Its upper displacement bound gives $m_i\geq c_1/(12N)\geq m_*$, proving <a href="#sc:outward-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:outward-weight">[sc:outward-weight]</a>.

The parameter inequality and nonnegativity of the outer template give $m_iB_o(s_i)\leq1$, proving its cap bound. The lower level bound is already known. The positive norm increment implies $s_i\leq s+\zeta/2<3/4+1/32<1-\eta$. Thus the selected endpoint lies in the region where <a href="#sc:curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:curvature">[sc:curvature]</a> applies when $M_0=2/m_*$. ◻

</details>

For a convergent sequence of active representations, an endpoint selected by Lemma <a href="#sc:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:outward">25</a> in the limit carries a definite positive moment as well as a definite weight. Its strict outward displacement and its strict separation from the right region persist for the corresponding approximating vertices. The remaining analytic task is to produce the one-dimensional difference space and a bound $q^2\geq\kappa D$ with $\kappa>0$ independent of $K$. Together with <a href="#sc:outward-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:outward-norm">[sc:outward-norm]</a>, these are the hypotheses of Corollary <a href="#spec:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:outward">16</a>, which will compare the center and selected outer Jacobians.

<a id="sec:jets"></a>
### Lower jets and the determinant contradiction

The comparison maxima of Section <a href="#sc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:section">4</a> relate a center endpoint to finitely many active outer endpoints. We seek determinant bounds for lower tests of the original potential at these endpoints. The main obstacle is that the center selected by a maximum need not belong to the full-measure set where the original transport Jacobian equation holds. Even though the short-time minimizing map is Lipschitz, it could send a set of positive volume into that exceptional set.

We first transfer lower jets from the prefix regularization to its active outer endpoints. The original contact-volume bound applies to these lower tests and bounds the selected outer determinant. That bound controls the regularized center Hessian, which in turn makes the short-time minimizing map nonsingular at the sampled points. Only then can we use the original Jacobian equation at the centers. The resulting matrix inequalities give a determinant gain proportional to the outer curvature parameter $K$, contradicting the geometric comparison from Section <a href="#sec:spectral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:spectral">3</a>.

Throughout the argument the manifold and density bounds are fixed. In the local calculations, $F,G$ and their indexed versions denote regularized potentials; the scalar oscillation function $F$ from Section <a href="#sc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:section">4</a> returns in Proposition <a href="#jet:contradiction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:contradiction">35</a>.

Throughout this section, a *lower Taylor jet* $(\zeta,B)$ of a function $f$ at the origin means $$f(h)\ge f(0)+\zeta\cdot h+\tfrac12 B(h,h)+o(|h|^2).$$ Replacing $B$ by $B-\varepsilon\mathrm{Id}$, for any $\varepsilon>0$, gives a genuine quadratic lower test on a sufficiently small neighborhood. All the tests to which we apply a determinant inequality will have a strictly positive relative Hessian, so this replacement and the subsequent limit $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ are harmless.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The original contact measure and lower tests

<a id="stmt-jet-contact-volume"></a>
<div id="jet:contact-volume" class="lemma">

<a id="jet:contact-volume-eq"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Contact-volume comparison). *Let $u,v$ be the dual potentials for an admissible pair of densities, and let $$\mathcal C=\{(x,y):u(x)+c(x,y)+v(y)=0\}.$$ For a Borel set $A$ on either side, its contact image satisfies $$\label{jet:contact-volume-eq}
 \frac{\lambda}{\Lambda}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(A)
 \le \mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(\mathcal C(A))
 \le \frac{\Lambda}{\lambda}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(A).$$ Projections are interpreted in the completed volume measures.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-contact-volume"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-contact-volume</summary>

*Proof.* The contact relation is closed. Its projections of Borel sets are analytic, hence measurable for the completed measures. The optimal coupling has full mass on $\mathcal C$. At every differentiability point of either potential its contact endpoint is unique: all minimizing first-variation logs must equal its gradient, and their exponential endpoints therefore coincide. Since both marginals are absolutely continuous, almost every contact pair lies in the sets where both potentials are differentiable. The two contact maps on these sets are inverse to each other.

Write $\mu=\rho_0\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}$ and $\nu=\rho_1\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}$. Consequently $\nu(\mathcal C(A))=\mu(A)$ for sets on the first side, and $\mu(\mathcal C(A))=\nu(A)$ for sets on the second side: any additional contact image outside the almost-everywhere map image consists, up to a null set, of points where the opposite potential is not differentiable. The density bounds give <a href="#jet:contact-volume-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:contact-volume-eq">[jet:contact-volume-eq]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-jet-viscosity"></a>
<div id="jet:viscosity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Upper determinant bound for a lower test). *Let $v$ be one of the original dual potentials. Suppose a $C^2$ function $\phi$ touches $v$ from below at $y$, its gradient $r=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\phi(y)$ belongs to $I(y)$, and $$W=\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}\phi(y)+A_y(r)>0.$$ Then <a id="jet:viscosity-eq"></a>$$\label{jet:viscosity-eq}
 \sigma_y(r)\det W\le \Lambda/\lambda.$$ The same conclusion holds for a lower Taylor jet with these properties, by approximation of its quadratic part.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-viscosity"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-viscosity</summary>

<a id="jet:smooth-jacobian"></a>
*Proof.* It suffices first to prove the result after decreasing the Hessian by a small positive multiple of the metric. Thus we may arrange strict touching, with $v-\phi\ge\varepsilon d(\cdot,y)^2$ in a small coordinate ball. The map $$S(z)=\exp_z\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\phi(z)$$ is a local diffeomorphism near $y$. Indeed its defining equation is $D_zc(z,S(z))+\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\phi(z)=0$, and differentiation gives $$\label{jet:smooth-jacobian}
 |\det DS(z)|
 =\sigma_z(\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\phi(z))
   \det\{\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}\phi(z)+A_z(\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\phi(z))\}.$$ The relative Hessian is positive throughout a sufficiently small ball, and the logs remain before cut.

For $h>0$, let $\Omega_h$ be the connected component containing $y$ of $\{\phi+h>v\}$ inside that ball. It is relatively compact and shrinks to $y$. If $z\in\Omega_h$ and $x=S(z)$, the cost mountain $$m_x(w)=\phi(z)+c(z,x)-c(w,x)$$ is tangent to $\phi$ at $z$. On a fixed smaller coordinate ball, $\phi-m_x$ has positive Hessian, uniformly for $x$ close to $S(y)$; thus $\phi\ge m_x$ there. On $\partial\Omega_h$ we have $v-m_x\ge h$, whereas at $z$ we have $v(z)-m_x(z)<h$. The minimum of $v-m_x$ on $\overline{\Omega_h}$ is therefore attained at an interior point $z'$. Thus $\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}m_x(z')\in\partial v(z')$. By Theorem <a href="#geo:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:support">6</a>, the corresponding local contact is a global cost contact. Its endpoint is $x$. This proves $$S(\Omega_h)\subset\mathcal C(\Omega_h).$$ Use the area formula for the diffeomorphism $S$ (Simon 2018, chap. 2, Theorem 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)], followed by Lemma <a href="#jet:contact-volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:contact-volume">26</a>, to obtain $$\inf_{\Omega_h}|\det DS|\,\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(\Omega_h)
 \le \mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(S(\Omega_h))
 \le(\Lambda/\lambda)\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(\Omega_h).$$ Let $h\downarrow0$ and then restore the original Hessian. ◻

</details>

<a id="jet:ae-jacobian"></a>
Only the original correspondence occurs in these two Lemmas. No density bound is claimed for a modified potential or its envelope. For later use, the almost-everywhere counterpart of <a href="#jet:smooth-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:smooth-jacobian">[jet:smooth-jacobian]</a> is the following consequence of (Cordero-Erausquin, McCann, and Schmuckenschläger 2001, Proposition 4.1 and Theorem 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[CEMS2001](#ref-CEMS2001)]. There is a volume-null Borel set $N\subset M$ such that, at every $y\notin N$, $v$ has an Alexandrov Hessian, its true log $r=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v(y)$ is before cut, and $$\label{jet:ae-jacobian}
 W_v(y):=\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}v(y)+A_y(r)>0,\qquad
 \frac{\lambda}{\Lambda}
 \le \sigma_y(r)\det W_v(y)
 \le\frac{\Lambda}{\lambda}.$$ We enlarge $N$ to include the exceptional sets in the contact-map differential of (Cordero-Erausquin, McCann, and Schmuckenschläger 2001, Proposition 4.1(b)) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[CEMS2001](#ref-CEMS2001)]. The Jacobian equation in (Cordero-Erausquin, McCann, and Schmuckenschläger 2001, Theorem 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[CEMS2001](#ref-CEMS2001)] assumes only absolute continuity with $L^1$ densities. The lower density bound makes its full-source-measure set a full-volume set; differentiability of a density is not required.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### An exact endpoint-jet calculation

Fix a continuous datum $w_o$ and put $$g(x)=\max_y\{-c(x,y)-w_o(y)\},\qquad F=Q_tg,\quad 0<t<1.$$ By Proposition <a href="#geo:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:intermediate">9</a>, $F$ is locally $C^{1,1}$ and its minimizing pole $x(z)$ is unique and locally Lipschitz. The following calculation concerns the first minimum alone.

<a id="stmt-jet-transfer"></a>
<div id="jet:transfer" class="lemma">

<a id="jet:L-properties"></a>
**Lemma 28** (The pole matrix and endpoint lower jets). *Suppose $z_0$ is a point where $F$ has a second-order expansion and $x(z)$ is differentiable. Write $x=x(z_0)$, $z_0=\exp_x(tp)$, and choose active logs $$p=\sum_{j=1}^Nm_jp_j,\qquad m_j>0,\quad
 \sum_jm_j=1,\qquad N\le n+1,$$ where $y_j=\exp_xp_j$ and $g(x)+c(x,y_j)+w_o(y_j)=0$. Set $E=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{p_j-p:1\le j\le N\}$. In the fixed-pole coordinates $z(q)=\exp_x(tq)$, define <a id="jet:L-definition"></a>$$\label{jet:L-definition}
 L=D_q^2\{c_t(x,z(q))-F(z(q))\}\big|_{q=p}.$$ Then $$\label{jet:L-properties}
 L\ge0,\qquad LE=0.$$ For every $j$, the function $$\Psi_j(d)=w_o(\exp_x(p_j+d))+\tfrac12|p_j+d|^2$$ has at $d=0$ the lower Taylor jet $(0,m_jL)$, after subtracting its value at zero. If $p_j$ is nonconjugate, this is an endpoint lower test in the smooth chosen cost branch.*

*More precisely, for $t<s<1$ put $$Q_s=\frac1tA_x(tp)-\frac1s\sum_jm_j A_x(sp_j).$$ Then $Q_s\ge0$ on $E^\perp$, and $m_j(L+LQ_sL)$ is a lower Taylor Hessian for $\Psi_j$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-transfer</summary>

<a id="jet:pole-identity"></a>
*Proof.* Use normal coordinates $x'=\exp_xa$ and set $$B(a,q)=c_t(\exp_xa,z(q)),\quad
 f(q)=F(z(q)),\quad a(q)=\log_xx(z(q)).$$ At the reference point $a(p)=0$. First variation gives the exact identities $$B(0,q)=\tfrac t2|q|^2,\qquad B_a(0,q)=-q.$$ Thus $B_{qq}=t\mathrm{Id}$, $B_{qa}=-\mathrm{Id}$ at $(0,p)$. The envelope identity $Df(q)=B_q(a(q),q)$ and differentiability of the pole imply $$\label{jet:pole-identity}
 D^2f=t\mathrm{Id}-R,\qquad R=D_qa(p)=L.$$ In particular $R$ is symmetric. Since $f(q)\le g(x)+B(0,q)$ with equality at $p$, we have $L\ge0$.

<a id="jet:split-cost"></a>
<a id="jet:split-derivatives"></a>
Choose a common $s\in(t,1)$. For each $j$ define $$y_j(d)=\exp_x(p_j+d),\qquad
 z_j(d)=\exp_x(s(p_j+d)),$$ and the split upper cost $$\label{jet:split-cost}
 C_j(a,d)=
 \frac{c(\exp_xa,z_j(d))}{s}
 +\frac{c(z_j(d),y_j(d))}{1-s}.$$ Both cost pieces are smooth near $(0,0)$ because they are proper subsegments of a minimizing ray. Energy splitting gives $c(\exp_xa,y_j(d))\le C_j(a,d)$. At $a=0$, the chosen geodesic remains minimizing on both pieces for small $d$, and consequently $$\label{jet:split-derivatives}
 C_j(0,d)=\tfrac12|p_j+d|^2,\quad
 (C_j)_a(0,d)=-(p_j+d),\quad
 (C_j)_{ad}=-\mathrm{Id},\quad
 (C_j)_{aa}=\frac1sA_x(sp_j).$$ The use of the fixed reference pole in $z_j(d)$ is what makes the mixed derivative exactly $-\mathrm{Id}$.

The nonnegative function $$g(x(z(q)))+C_j(a(q),0)+w_o(y_j)$$ vanishes at $p$. It is differentiable there, since $g(x(z(q)))=f(q)-B(a(q),q)$, and its derivative equals $(p-p_j)^*R$. This derivative is zero. Symmetry in <a href="#jet:pole-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:pole-identity">[jet:pole-identity]</a> proves $LE=0$.

On $E^\perp$, MTW concavity on the convex hull of the $sp_j$, followed by divided-action monotonicity, gives $$Q_s\ge \frac1tA_x(tp)-\frac1s A_x(sp)>0.$$ All points used in this inequality are before cut by Theorem <a href="#geo:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:support">6</a>; equivalently one may invoke Lemma <a href="#geo:slack" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:slack">4</a>. Notice that unsplit endpoints may still be conjugate.

Fix $i$ and write $m=m_i$. Given $d$, choose any linear map $U:T_xM\to E$, and set $k=m(d+Ud)$ and $a=a(p+k)$. In the lower bound defining $g(\exp_xa)$, move endpoint $i$ to $y_i(d)$, keep all other endpoints fixed, replace the costs by <a href="#jet:split-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:split-cost">[jet:split-cost]</a>, and average with the old weights. Write $\widehat\Psi_i(d)=\Psi_i(d)-\Psi_i(0)$. The resulting inequality is $$f(p+k)\ge
 B(a,p+k)-\sum_jm_j\{C_j(a,d_j)+w_o(y_j(d_j))\},$$ where $d_i=d$ and $d_j=0$ for $j\ne i$. Subtract the equality at $(a,k,d)=(0,0,0)$ and use <a href="#jet:pole-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:pole-identity">[jet:pole-identity]</a>–<a href="#jet:split-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:split-derivatives">[jet:split-derivatives]</a>. The linear terms cancel: the pole terms cancel because $\sum m_jp_j=p$, and the prefix terms cancel because $Df(p)=tp$. The remaining inequality is <a id="jet:quadratic-transfer"></a>$$\label{jet:quadratic-transfer}
 m\widehat\Psi_i(d)\ge
 \tfrac12L(k,k)+\tfrac12Q_s(a,a)
 +a\cdot(md-k)+o(|d|^2).$$ Here $a=Lk+o(|d|)$. Since $md-k=-mUd$ and $\operatorname{range}L\subset E^\perp$, the mixed term is $o(|d|^2)$. Hence the lower Hessian is $$m(\mathrm{Id}+U)^*(L+LQ_sL)(\mathrm{Id}+U).$$ In fact this equals $m(L+LQ_sL)$ because $LU=0$. The latter dominates $mL$, proving the asserted jet. The linear term is exactly zero independently of any second function or maximum condition.

The map $d\mapsto y_i(d)$ need not be invertible for the preceding pullback calculation. When $p_i$ is nonconjugate it is a local diffeomorphism, and $\tfrac12|p_i+d|^2$ is the smooth chosen upper branch of $c(x,y_i(d))$. This converts the pullback jet into an endpoint jet. ◻

</details>

<a id="jet:H-definition"></a>
<a id="jet:Jensen-error"></a>
Let $G$ be another locally $C^{1,1}$ function, and define $$\label{jet:H-definition}
 H=D_q^2\{c_t(x,z(q))+G(z(q))\}\big|_{q=p}.$$ The exact identity <a id="jet:H-minus-L"></a>$$\label{jet:H-minus-L}
 H-L=D_q^2\{(F+G)(z(q))\}\big|_{q=p}$$ will distinguish the first-minimum calculation from the maximum argument. At a twice differentiable maximum, it gives $H\le L$ and $H|_E\le0$. At a Jensen point with gradient and upper Hessian errors tending to zero in a fixed smooth chart, it gives $$\label{jet:Jensen-error}
 H\le L+\eta\mathrm{Id},\qquad \eta\longrightarrow0.$$ The coordinate-change term uses the gradient error and also tends to zero. Lemma <a href="#jet:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:transfer">28</a> is exact at such a point. For every linear $U$ with image in $E$, it therefore supplies a lower jet $H_i=m_iL$ satisfying $$H_i\ge m_i(\mathrm{Id}+U)^*H(\mathrm{Id}+U)
       -m_i\eta(\mathrm{Id}+U)^*(\mathrm{Id}+U).$$ In particular no approximate stationarity term occurs in the linear coefficient of the endpoint test.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The center semibound without a density equation

We use the scales and templates of the preceding section. To distinguish a scalar argument from a point of the manifold, write $$\varphi_c(h)=h+bB_c((h-a)/D),\quad
 \varphi_o(h)=h+bB_o((h-a)/D),\quad
 w_c=\varphi_c\circ v,\quad w_o=\varphi_o\circ v.$$ For each fixed outer template the scales are sufficiently small that both scalar maps have derivative between $1/2$ and $2$. On the fixed compact interval of possible center levels, $B_c'$ is negative and bounded away from zero. Its derivative bounds are fixed independently of the outer curvature parameter $K$.

<a id="stmt-jet-semibound"></a>
<div id="jet:semibound" class="lemma">

<a id="jet:semibound-eq"></a>
**Lemma 29** (A lower semibound before the center Jacobian). *Fix one set of scales, one original potential $v$, and the two templates. Let $t_j\uparrow1$ and $b_j\in[b_-,b_+]$. Put $$F_j=Q_{t_j}g_{b_j},\qquad G_j=Q_{1-t_j}w_{c,b_j}.$$ Let $z_j$ be simultaneous differentiability points for the objects in Lemma <a href="#jet:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:transfer">28</a>, with second-order expansions of $G_j$, whose center levels lie in the fixed compact interval above. Suppose $$|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}(F_j+G_j)(z_j)|\longrightarrow0.$$ Let $x_j$ be the prefix pole and $z_j=\exp_{x_j}(t_jp_j)$. The matrix $H_j$ of <a href="#jet:H-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:H-definition">[jet:H-definition]</a> satisfies $$\label{jet:semibound-eq}
 H_j\ge c\frac{b_j}{D}\mathrm{Id}
       -C\frac{b_j}{D^2}p_jp_j^*-\varepsilon_j\mathrm{Id},
 \qquad \varepsilon_j\longrightarrow0.$$ The constants $c,C$ depend on the geometry and the fixed center template, but not on $K$. The errors tend to zero with the prefix and gradient errors at the fixed scales. No upper bound on $H_j$, and no Alexandrov Hessian of $v$ at the center, is assumed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-semibound"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-semibound</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\tau_j=1-t_j$, and let $y_j$ be the unique final minimizer. Set $$r_j=\tau_j^{-1}\log_{y_j}z_j,\qquad
 l_j=\varphi_c'(v(y_j))<1.$$ The short minimum gives a smooth local lower test for $w_c$ with gradient $r_j$. Inverting $\varphi_c$ gives a local lower test for $v$ with gradient $r_j/l_j$. Theorem <a href="#geo:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:support">6</a> therefore implies that $$\zeta_j=\exp_{y_j}(r_j/l_j)$$ is a true contact endpoint of $v$, joined by the indicated minimizing ray. In particular $\widehat x_j=\exp_{y_j}r_j$ is strictly before cut along that ray.

<a id="jet:center-support-jet"></a>
For the moment omit subscripts. Put $\theta=(1+l^{-1})/2$ and $\xi=\exp_y(\theta r)$. Split the true support at $\xi$, using the fraction $l\theta$ of its unit duration. The resulting upper cost support is $$C(h)=\frac{c(h,\xi)}{l\theta}
       +\frac{c(\xi,\zeta)}{1-l\theta}.$$ It agrees with $c(h,\zeta)$ at $h=y$, and $v(h)\ge
v(y)+c(y,\zeta)-C(h)$. Thus $$\psi(h)=\varphi_c\{v(y)+c(y,\zeta)-C(h)\}$$ is a smooth local lower support for $w_c$, with $$\label{jet:center-support-jet}
 \mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\psi(y)=r,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}\psi(y)
 =-\theta^{-1}A_y(\theta r)
   +\frac{\varphi_c''(v(y))}{l^2}rr^*.$$ The original true endpoint $\zeta$ may be conjugate. The point $\xi$ is halfway into the available extension and is before cut, which is all that this construction uses.

<a id="jet:smooth-short-limit"></a>
At the fixed scales, $1-l$ has a positive lower bound and the parameters $(y,r,l,v(y))$ range over a compact set. The first cost piece in $C$ is uniformly smooth on a fixed neighborhood of $y$; the second piece is constant in $h$. Consequently the supports $\psi$ have uniform smooth bounds and a uniform supporting radius. For sufficiently small $\tau$, the local infimum $Q_\tau\psi$ is smooth near $z$, has minimizer $y$ at $z$, and supports $Q_\tau w_c$ from below there. To justify the last assertion, all minimizers of the latter lie within $O(\tau)$ of $z$, by the Lipschitz bound on $w_c$, and so lie in the supporting neighborhood. For $\psi$ the local Hessian of the minimizing expression is $\tau^{-1}\mathrm{Id}+O(1)>0$. Equality and stationary first variation hold at $y$. The implicit-function theorem and the Schur formula for a minimum give, after parallel identification, $$\label{jet:smooth-short-limit}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}Q_\tau\psi(z)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}\psi(y)+O(\tau).$$ All constants in this statement are uniform for the fixed-scale family just described.

<a id="jet:center-support-pullback"></a>
Let $\widehat p=\log_{\widehat x}y$ and $\widehat L=(d\exp_{\widehat x})_{\widehat p}$. Lemma <a href="#spec:pullback" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:pullback">14</a>, applied to the minimizing ray through duration $1/l$, gives $$\widehat L^*
 \{A_y(r)-\theta^{-1}A_y(\theta r)\}\widehat L
 \ge c(1-l)\mathrm{Id}.$$ Gauss’s Lemma gives $\widehat L^*r=-\widehat p$. Combining this with <a href="#jet:center-support-jet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:center-support-jet">[jet:center-support-jet]</a> yields $$\label{jet:center-support-pullback}
 \widehat L^*\{\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}\psi(y)+A_y(r)\}\widehat L
 \ge c(1-l)\mathrm{Id}
       +\frac{\varphi_c''(v(y))}{l^2}
          \widehat p\widehat p^*.$$ Here $1-l\ge c b/D$ and $|\varphi_c''(v(y))|/l^2\le Cb/D^2$.

We finally compare the reference pole with $\widehat x_j$. The envelope identities give $$x_j=\exp_{z_j}(-t_j\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}F_j(z_j)),\qquad
 \widehat x_j=\exp_{z_j}(t_j\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}G_j(z_j)).$$ They are at distance tending to zero. The pairs $(\widehat x_j,y_j)$ have a uniform fixed-scale extension, so their cost charts and inverse exponential charts are uniformly smooth. Omit subscripts again and put $U=Q_\tau\psi$, $Z(q)=\exp_x(tq)$, $M=D_qZ(p)$, and $R=\nabla_q^2Z(p)$. Since $G-U$ has a local minimum at $z$, its first derivative vanishes and its second-order form is positive semidefinite. Consequently $$H\ge H_\psi
 :=t\mathrm{Id}+M^*\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}U(z)M+\langle\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}U(z),R\rangle.$$ Only the smooth support $U$ occurs on the right. Its gradient and Hessian are uniformly bounded at the fixed scales, and <a href="#jet:smooth-short-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:smooth-short-limit">[jet:smooth-short-limit]</a> identifies its Hessian with that of $\psi$, up to $O(\tau)$ after parallel transport.

Write $\delta=|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}(F+G)(z)|$ and identify nearby pole tangent spaces by parallel transport. The explicit pole formulas give $d(x,\widehat x)=O(\delta)$. Moreover $p=t^{-1}\log_xz$ and $\widehat p=t^{-1}\log_{\widehat x}z$ differ by $O_{\mathrm{fixed}}(\delta)$, since the latter ray has a uniform fixed-scale extension. Thus replacing $x,p$ by $\widehat x,\widehat p$ in $M,R$ changes their entries by $O_{\mathrm{fixed}}(\delta)$. Changing $t$ to $1$ then changes them by $O(\tau)$. Every resulting error in $H_\psi$ multiplies a bounded derivative of $U$.

For the full endpoint map $Y(q)=\exp_{\widehat x}q$, let $B=DY(\widehat p)$. The identity $c(\widehat x,Y(q))=|q|^2/2$ on the chosen branch gives $$\mathrm{Id}=B^*A_y(r)B-\langle r,\nabla_q^2Y(\widehat p)\rangle.$$ Since $\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}\psi(y)=r$, the preceding expansions yield $$H\ge H_\psi
 =B^*\{\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}\psi(y)+A_y(r)\}B
       +O_{\mathrm{fixed}}(\tau+\delta).$$ Combining this with <a href="#jet:center-support-pullback" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:center-support-pullback">[jet:center-support-pullback]</a> proves <a href="#jet:semibound-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:semibound-eq">[jet:semibound-eq]</a>. No coordinate error multiplies the unknown Hessian of $G$. ◻

</details>

The true and modified endpoints occur in opposite orders along the center and outer reverse rays. In the center calculation, $l<1$ makes $\widehat x=\exp_y r$ a proper intermediate point on the true ray to $\zeta=\exp_y(r/l)$. At an active outer endpoint $y_i$, a slope $l_i=\varphi_o'(v(y_i))>1$ instead makes the true endpoint $\zeta_i=\exp_{y_i}(r_i/l_i)$ an intermediate point on the modified ray to $x=\exp_{y_i}r_i$, where $r_i$ is its minimizing reverse log. Figure <a href="#fig:reverse-rays" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:reverse-rays">1</a> displays this distinction. We next use it to exclude conjugacy at the full modified outer endpoint.

<figure id="fig:reverse-rays">
<div class="picture">
<p>(128,57) (0,50)<span>(128,5)[l]<span>Center modification: $l < 1$</span></span> (9,38)<span>(1,0)<span>110</span></span> (13,38) (72,38) (107,38) (8,40)<span>(10,5)<span>$y$</span></span> (67,40)<span>(10,5)<span>$x̂$</span></span> (102,40)<span>(10,5)<span>$ζ$</span></span> (8,31)<span>(10,5)<span>$0$</span></span> (67,31)<span>(10,5)<span>$1$</span></span> (97,31)<span>(20,5)<span>$1/l > 1$</span></span> (0,22)<span>(128,5)[l]<span>Selected outer modification: $l_{i} > 1$</span></span> (9,10)<span>(1,0)<span>110</span></span> (13,10) (58,10) (107,10) (8,12)<span>(10,5)<span>$y_{i}$</span></span> (53,12)<span>(10,5)<span>$ζ_{i}$</span></span> (102,12)<span>(10,5)<span>$x$</span></span> (8,3)<span>(10,5)<span>$0$</span></span> (43,3)<span>(30,5)<span>$1/l_{i} < 1$</span></span> (102,3)<span>(10,5)<span>$1$</span></span></p>
</div>
<figcaption>Two independent reverse rays, shown schematically with their duration parameters. The modified center endpoint $x̂$ and the true outer endpoint $ζ_{i}$ are strictly before cut. The full endpoints $ζ$ and $x$ may still be conjugate at this stage of the argument. The two rows are not drawn to a common scale.</figcaption>
</figure>

```text
  - $y$ with coordinate $0$
  - $\widehat{x}$ with coordinate $1$
  - $\zeta$ with coordinate $1/l > 1$
- Bottom row labeled "Selected outer modification: $l_i > 1$": A horizontal line ending with a right arrow, containing three marked points:
  - $y_i$ with coordinate $0$
  - $\zeta_i$ with coordinate $1/l_i < 1$
  - $x$ with coordinate $1$
```


<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### The outward endpoint and conjugacy

<a id="stmt-jet-outer-conjugacy"></a>
<div id="jet:outer-conjugacy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Conjugacy is excluded by an active increasing modification). *Fix $b>0$ and an active outer pair $$g_b(x)+c(x,y)+\varphi_o(v(y))=0.$$ Choose any minimizing reverse log $r$ from $y$ to $x$. Suppose $$l=\varphi_o'(v(y))>1,\qquad \varphi_o''(v(y))<0.$$ Then the chosen modified ray is nonconjugate at its endpoint. The true log $r/l$ is before cut.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-outer-conjugacy"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-outer-conjugacy</summary>

*Proof.* The active inequality reads $\varphi_o(v(h))\ge-c(x,h)-g_b(x)$. For $1/l<s<1$, put $\xi_s=\exp_y(sr)$ and form the smooth upper support $$C_s(h)=c(h,\xi_s)/s+c(\xi_s,x)/(1-s).$$ It has value $c(y,x)$ and gradient $-r$ at $y$. Since $\varphi_o$ is increasing, $\varphi_o^{-1}(-C_s-g_b(x))$ is a smooth lower test for $v$ with gradient $r/l$. That log is strictly before cut because it shortens a minimizing ray. It also gives a global contact by Theorem <a href="#geo:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:support">6</a>, but its before-cut property does not depend on a density equation.

The scaled true relative Hessian of this test is <a id="jet:outer-truncated"></a>$$\label{jet:outer-truncated}
 W_s=lA_y(r/l)-s^{-1}A_y(sr)
       -\frac{\varphi_o''(v(y))}{l^2}rr^*.$$ Fix $s_0\in(1/l,1)$. For $s\ge s_0$, divided-action monotonicity gives a positive definite lower bound for the difference of the first two terms, independent of $s$. The last term is positive semidefinite. If the endpoint at duration one were conjugate, the index-form characterization of conjugacy, equivalently Lemma <a href="#spec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:radial">12</a>, would force an eigenvalue of $s^{-1}A_y(sr)$ to tend to $-\infty$ as $s\uparrow1$. Thus $\det W_s\to\infty$, while $W_s$ retains a positive definite baseline. Lemma <a href="#jet:viscosity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:viscosity">27</a>, at the fixed true log $r/l$, instead gives $$\det W_s\le l^n(\Lambda/\lambda)/\sigma_y(r/l)<\infty.$$ This is a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-jet-outer-test"></a>
<div id="jet:outer-test" class="lemma">

<a id="jet:outer-form"></a>
<a id="jet:outer-det"></a>
**Lemma 31** (Undoing the outer modification). *At a pair as in Lemma <a href="#jet:outer-conjugacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:outer-conjugacy">30</a>, let $p=\log_xy$ be the corresponding forward log, $B=(d\exp_x)_p$, and let $H_o\ge0$ be a lower Taylor Hessian, with zero linear part, of $d\mapsto w_o(\exp_x(p+d))+\tfrac12|p+d|^2$. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 V_o={}&H_o+B^*\{lA_y(r/l)-A_y(r)\}B
             -\frac{\varphi_o''(v(y))}{l^2}pp^* .
 \label{jet:outer-form}
\end{aligned}$$ Then $V_o>0$ and $$\label{jet:outer-det}
 \det V_o\le
 l^n(\Lambda/\lambda)\,
       \frac{\sigma_x(p)^2}{\sigma_y(r/l)}.$$ The middle term in <a href="#jet:outer-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:outer-form">[jet:outer-form]</a> is positive definite. On a compact family of active pairs where $b>0$ is bounded away from zero, $\varphi_o'>1$ and $\varphi_o''<0$ hold with fixed strict margins, this term has a uniform positive definite baseline and the chosen exponential branches are uniformly nonsingular.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-outer-test"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-outer-test</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#jet:outer-conjugacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:outer-conjugacy">30</a> makes the endpoint coordinate map locally invertible. Its smooth cost branch has endpoint Hessian $A_y(r)$, and the zero linear coefficient fixes the gradient of the induced lower test of $w_o$ to be $r$. Inverting $\varphi_o$ and using the chain rule shows that <a href="#jet:outer-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:outer-form">[jet:outer-form]</a> is $l$ times the true relative lower-test Hessian, pulled back by $B$. Gauss’s Lemma gives the rank-one term displayed there.

The divided-action difference between durations $1/l$ and $1$ is strictly positive definite, so $V_o>0$. Decrease the lower Taylor Hessian by $\varepsilon\mathrm{Id}$ to obtain a genuine lower test, apply Lemma <a href="#jet:viscosity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:viscosity">27</a>, and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. Taking the determinant of the scaling and pullback gives <a href="#jet:outer-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:outer-det">[jet:outer-det]</a>.

For the uniformity assertion, include the minimizing log among the parameters. The family is compact and active contact is a closed condition. Lemma <a href="#jet:outer-conjugacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:outer-conjugacy">30</a> excludes a conjugate limit anywhere in that family; radial shortening excludes true cut limits. The smooth branch matrices and the positive divided-action difference therefore have the claimed uniform bounds by compactness. Multiple ordinary branches cause no difficulty: each specified nonconjugate minimizing branch is a smooth upper branch and has been included among the parameters. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The order of the limiting argument

We now select points at which the preceding lower tests and the original Jacobian equation can be combined. The following elementary fact will justify differentiation through a short-time minimizer once its endpoint is an Alexandrov point: neighboring endpoints need not be differentiable points of the datum.

<a id="stmt-jet-subgradient-differential"></a>
<div id="jet:subgradient-differential" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32** (Subgradients at an Alexandrov point). *If $f$ is semiconvex near $0$ and has the Alexandrov expansion $$f(h)=f(0)+p\cdot h+\tfrac12 A(h,h)+o(|h|^2),$$ then every nearby subgradient satisfies $$\zeta=p+Ah+o(|h|),\qquad \zeta\in\partial f(h),$$ uniformly over the choice of $\zeta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-subgradient-differential"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-subgradient-differential</summary>

*Proof.* Adding a fixed quadratic reduces the assertion to a convex function, and subtracting it later restores the displayed formula. In the convex case write the remainder as $R(h)$, where $|R(h)|\le\omega(|h|)|h|^2$ for a nondecreasing modulus $\omega(r)\to0$. For $r=|h|>0$, a unit vector $e$, and $0<\alpha\le1$, the subgradient inequalities at $h\pm\alpha r e$ give $$|(\zeta-p-Ah)\cdot e|
 \le \tfrac12\|A\|\alpha r
        +5\omega(2r)r/\alpha.$$ For small $r$, choose $\alpha=\sqrt{\max\{\omega(2r),r^2\}}$ and take the supremum over unit $e$. This proves the uniform assertion. ◻

</details>

The selection has two stages. At each fixed prefix time we first pass from nearby twice differentiable points to an exact maximum, where the parameter inequality holds for every active representation. After selecting an outer endpoint in that limit, we return to the nearby points to obtain the Hessian bound and the original center Jacobian. This order preserves the parameter inequality without assuming that a prescribed active vertex persists under perturbation.

<a id="stmt-jet-determinants"></a>
<div id="jet:determinants" class="proposition">

<a id="jet:limiting-geometry"></a>
<a id="jet:limiting-outward"></a>
<a id="jet:limiting-center"></a>
<a id="jet:limiting-outer"></a>
<a id="jet:limiting-determinants"></a>
**Proposition 33** (The limiting matrix configuration). *Fix the center template and the constants of Lemmas <a href="#sc:excess" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:excess">21</a>, <a href="#sc:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:maximum">22</a>, <a href="#sc:parameter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:parameter">23</a>, and <a href="#sc:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:outward">25</a>. Choose $K$, then an outer template with curvature less than $-K$ in the prescribed capped region. For all sufficiently small scales in the sequence of Lemma <a href="#sc:plateau" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:plateau">18</a>, there are a parameter $b\in[b_-,b_+]$, a pole $x$, center and active logs $p,p_i\in G_x$, weights $m_i>0$ summing to one, and symmetric matrices $H,L,V,V_i$ on $T_xM$, with the following properties. For one selected index $i$, $$\begin{gathered}
 p=\sum_jm_jp_j,\qquad
 E=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{p_j-p\}=\mathbb Re,\qquad |e|=1,\quad
 q=p\cdot e>0,\quad q^2\ge cD, \label{jet:limiting-geometry}\\
 m_i\ge m_*>0,\qquad p_i=p+d_ie,\quad d_i>0, \label{jet:limiting-outward}\\
 L\ge0,\qquad LE=0,\qquad H\le L,\qquad
 H\ge V+c\frac bD\mathrm{Id}-C\frac b{D^2}pp^*,\qquad V>0,
 \label{jet:limiting-center}\\
 V_i\ge m_iL+cK\frac b{D^2}p_ip_i^*,\qquad V_i>0.
 \label{jet:limiting-outer}
\end{gathered}$$ The center modified log and the selected outer modified log are nonconjugate. Let $r$ and $r_i$ denote the reverse logs of these specified rays, let $y=\exp_xp$, $y_i=\exp_xp_i$, and set $l=\varphi_c'(v(y))$, $l_i=\varphi_o'(v(y_i))$. The chosen true limiting branches are also nonconjugate, and $$\label{jet:limiting-determinants}
 \det V\ge c\,\frac{\sigma_x(p)^2}{\sigma_y(r/l)},
 \qquad
 \det V_i\le C\,\frac{\sigma_x(p_i)^2}{\sigma_{y_i}(r_i/l_i)}.$$ Here $c,C,m_*$ are independent of $K$ and the outer template. The level and norm-increment conclusions of Lemma <a href="#sc:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:outward">25</a> hold as well.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-determinants"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-determinants</summary>

*Proof.* *Step 1: fixed scales and exact maxima.* Throughout the proof the original potential $v$, the scales, and both templates are fixed. Constants used only to pass limits may depend on all of them. Choose $t_k\uparrow1$ and interior maxima $(z_k^0,b_k)$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#sc:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:maximum">22</a>, for $$F_k(z)=Q_{t_k}g_{b_k}(z),\qquad
 G_k(z)=Q_{1-t_k}w_{c,b_k}(z).$$ The functions are $C^{1,1}$ in space: Proposition <a href="#geo:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:intermediate">9</a> applies to the first, and Lemma <a href="#geo:short-time" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:short-time">5</a> to the second. At an exact maximum the velocities align. If $x_k^0$ and $y_k^0$ are its two minimizers and $p_k^0=t_k^{-1}\log_{x_k^0}z_k^0$, then $y_k^0=\exp_{x_k^0}p_k^0$ along the concatenated minimizing ray. Every finite positive active representation of $p_k^0$ satisfies the parameter inequality of Lemma <a href="#sc:parameter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:parameter">23</a>. The level relations and common section bounds of Lemma <a href="#sc:levels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:levels">24</a> hold for these representations.

<a id="jet:null-preimage"></a>
*Step 2: null sets are removed before Jensen sampling.* Let $N$ be the null set for the original potential in <a href="#jet:ae-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:ae-jacobian">[jet:ae-jacobian]</a>. For this fixed $k$, the final minimizer map $Y_k(z)$ is locally Lipschitz and hence differentiable almost everywhere (Simon 2018, chap. 2, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)]. In each coordinate chart the locally Lipschitz area formula (Simon 2018, chap. 2, Theorem 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)] implies that $$\label{jet:null-preimage}
 Z_k=\{z:DY_k(z)\text{ exists},\ \det DY_k(z)\ne0,\
                     Y_k(z)\in N\}$$ is a null set. Indeed the integral of $|\det DY_k|$ on $Y_k^{-1}(N)$ is zero. This argument does not require us to know in advance where $DY_k$ is nonsingular.

<a id="jet:Jensen-sequence"></a>
Apply the semiconvex maximum Lemma (Crandall, Ishii, and Lions 1992, Appendix, Lemmas A.3–A.4) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[CIL1992](#ref-CIL1992)] to $F_k+G_k$ at $z_k^0$, using a small fourth-order strictness penalty if needed. Its positive-measure conclusion allows us to choose a sequence $z_{k,\nu}\to z_k^0$ off $Z_k$ and off all the null sets where $F_k,G_k$, the prefix pole map, or $Y_k$ lack the derivatives used below. We have $$\label{jet:Jensen-sequence}
 \mathop{\mathrm{grad}}(F_k+G_k)(z_{k,\nu})\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 D^2(F_k+G_k)(z_{k,\nu})\le \eta_{k,\nu}\mathrm{Id},\quad
 \eta_{k,\nu}\longrightarrow0$$ in the fixed chart. At every such point choose a representation with at most $n+1$ active logs, padding by zero weights only for compactness. Lemma <a href="#jet:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:transfer">28</a> applies at each point. Its matrices $L_{k,\nu}$ are positive semidefinite and annihilate the active differences, exactly; its endpoint lower jets are $m_{i,k,\nu}
L_{k,\nu}$. The matrices $H_{k,\nu}$ satisfy <a href="#jet:Jensen-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:Jensen-error">[jet:Jensen-error]</a>.

<a id="jet:fixed-prefix-limits"></a>
<a id="jet:absorbed-semibound"></a>
*Step 3: take the first, fixed-prefix limits.* For each fixed $k$, the local $C^{1,1}$ bounds are finite. After taking a subsequence in $\nu$, all configuration parameters, weights, and the two matrices $H_{k,\nu},L_{k,\nu}$ converge. Denote the limits by $p_k,p_{i,k},m_{i,k},H_k,L_k$ and the corresponding base points. Discard zero limiting weights. The limiting endpoints are active at the exact maximum, and their weighted sum is $p_k$. In particular the *universal* parameter inequality applies to this limiting representation; we have not asserted forward persistence of any prescribed active vertex. We retain $$\label{jet:fixed-prefix-limits}
 L_k\ge0,\qquad L_kE_k=0,\qquad H_k\le L_k,
 \quad E_k=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}\{p_{i,k}-p_k:m_{i,k}>0\}.$$ The quantitative proof of Lemma <a href="#jet:semibound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:semibound">29</a> has an error bounded by a fixed-scale constant times $1-t_k+|\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}(F_k+G_k)(z_{k,\nu})|$. Passing $\nu\to\infty$ therefore gives $$H_k\ge c\frac{b_k}{D}\mathrm{Id}
           -C\frac{b_k}{D^2}p_kp_k^*
           -C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(1-t_k)\mathrm{Id}.$$ Since $b_k\ge b_->0$, for sufficiently large $k$ this implies, after reducing $c$, $$\label{jet:absorbed-semibound}
 H_k\ge c\frac{b_k}{D}\mathrm{Id}
           -C\frac{b_k}{D^2}p_kp_k^*.$$

If $\dim E_k\ge2$, a nonzero vector in $E_k\cap p_k^\perp$ contradicts <a href="#jet:fixed-prefix-limits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:fixed-prefix-limits">[jet:fixed-prefix-limits]</a> and <a href="#jet:absorbed-semibound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:absorbed-semibound">[jet:absorbed-semibound]</a>. If $E_k=0$, all active logs in the representation equal $p_k$ and all endpoints equal the center. But $B_c\le B_o$ at every level, contradicting the strict parameter inequality. Thus $E_k=\mathbb Re_k$. Orient $e_k$ so $q_k=p_k\cdot e_k>0$; the same two inequalities give $q_k^2\ge cD$. Lemma <a href="#sc:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:outward">25</a> now supplies an index with $m_{i,k}\ge m_*$, a strict outward displacement, and a bounded template value. We choose an index carrying a fixed share of the outward moment, as permitted by that Lemma, so its displacement has a positive lower bound at the fixed scales. Relabel it as index $1$.

*Step 4: fix the outer branch before bounding any center Hessian.* Pass to a subsequence in $k$ so that the selected configurations and their weights converge. Their centers and selected endpoints remain in the compact level ranges of Lemma <a href="#sc:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:outward">25</a>. The selected limiting weight is at least $m_*$, its displacement is strictly outward, and its outer template value is below the prescribed cap. By imposing the template conditions under a slightly larger fixed cap, the limiting slope and curvature conditions have strict margins: $B_o'>0$ and $B_o''<-K$. The limiting selected pair is active. Lemma <a href="#jet:outer-conjugacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:outer-conjugacy">30</a> excludes conjugacy of its modified branch, using only this activity and the original contact-volume bound. Its shortened true log is before cut. Lemma <a href="#jet:outer-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:outer-test">31</a> consequently supplies a neighborhood of this configuration with constants $\delta>0$ and $M<\infty$ such that its divided-action gain is at least $\delta\mathrm{Id}$ and its determinant upper bound is at most $M$. These constants may depend on the fixed scales and templates.

For every remaining $k$, return to its already chosen Jensen subsequence and choose $\nu=\nu(k)$ sufficiently large. We require the configuration and matrices to be within $1/k$ of their fixed-prefix limits, the selected weight to be at least $m_*/2$, and the selected endpoint to lie in that neighborhood. We also require both errors in <a href="#jet:Jensen-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:Jensen-sequence">[jet:Jensen-sequence]</a>, including their transformed versions in <a href="#jet:Jensen-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:Jensen-error">[jet:Jensen-error]</a>, to be at most $(1-t_k)^3/k$. All these requirements are compatible because the limit in $\nu$ is taken with $k$ fixed. For the rest of the proof, a subscript $k$ denotes this diagonal sequence of actual Jensen points.

*Step 5: the outer test bounds the exact pole matrix.* At each diagonal point, Lemma <a href="#jet:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:transfer">28</a> gives the actual lower Taylor Hessian $H_{o,k}=m_{1,k}L_k\ge0$ at the selected endpoint. Use Lemma <a href="#jet:outer-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:outer-test">31</a> to undo the modification and call the resulting form $V_{1,k}$. The positive outer curvature term and the uniform divided-action baseline give <a id="jet:bootstrap"></a>$$\label{jet:bootstrap}
 V_{1,k}\ge m_{1,k}L_k+\delta\mathrm{Id},\qquad
 \det V_{1,k}\le M.$$ Every eigenvalue of $V_{1,k}$ is at least $\delta$, so its largest eigenvalue is at most $M\delta^{1-n}$. Since $m_{1,k}\ge m_*/2$, this bounds $L_k$ from above. The comparison $H_k\le L_k+o(1)\mathrm{Id}$ and Lemma <a href="#jet:semibound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:semibound">29</a> now bound $H_k$ from both sides. There has still been no use of a center density equation.

<a id="jet:G-bound"></a>
The center extension gives uniformly nonsingular fixed-scale prefix-log coordinate maps, as in the proof of Lemma <a href="#jet:semibound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:semibound">29</a>. Their derivatives and second derivatives are bounded; the gradients of $G_k$ are bounded by the Lipschitz bound of the datum. Thus the coordinate change in <a href="#jet:H-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:H-definition">[jet:H-definition]</a> converts the bound on $H_k$ into $$\label{jet:G-bound}
 \|\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}G_k(z_k)\|\le C_{\mathrm{fixed}}.$$

<a id="jet:Y-identity"></a>
*Step 6: the chosen centers are good original endpoints.* Write $\tau_k=1-t_k$, $y_k=Y_k(z_k)$, and $K_k(z,y)=c(z,y)/\tau_k$. The envelope identity $\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}G_k=(K_k)_z(z,Y_k(z))$ and differentiability of $Y_k$ give $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}G_k=(K_k)_{zz}+(K_k)_{zy}DY_k.$$ In parallel orthonormal frames, the distance between $z_k$ and $y_k$ is $O(\tau_k)$, and the smooth short-cost expansions are $$(K_k)_{zz}=\tau_k^{-1}\mathrm{Id}+O(\tau_k),\quad
 (K_k)_{yy}=\tau_k^{-1}\mathrm{Id}+O(\tau_k),\quad
 (K_k)_{zy}=-\tau_k^{-1}\Pi+O(\tau_k),$$ where $\Pi$ is the appropriate parallel isometry. Solving the preceding identity, using <a href="#jet:G-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:G-bound">[jet:G-bound]</a>, gives $$\label{jet:Y-identity}
 DY_k=\Pi^{-1}\{\mathrm{Id}-\tau_k\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}G_k\}+O(\tau_k^2).$$ It is nonsingular for large $k$. The Jensen points were chosen off the set <a href="#jet:null-preimage" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:null-preimage">[jet:null-preimage]</a>; hence $y_k\notin N$. This is the promised justification for using the original Alexandrov and Jacobian statements at the centers.

<a id="jet:undo-short"></a>
In the following formulas the scalar modifications are evaluated with $b=b_k$. At these points $w_c=\varphi_c\circ v$ has an Alexandrov Hessian. The minimizing property supplies a proximal subgradient $$\xi(z)=-(K_k)_y(z,Y_k(z))\in\partial w_c(Y_k(z))$$ at every nearby point, even if $w_c$ is not differentiable there. Apply Lemma <a href="#jet:subgradient-differential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:subgradient-differential">32</a> at the Alexandrov point $y_k=Y_k(z_k)$. Since $Y_k$ is Lipschitz and differentiable at $z_k$, it gives, in normal coordinates at $z_k$ and $y_k$, $$\xi(z_k+h)=\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}w_c(y_k)
       +\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}w_c(y_k)DY_k(z_k)h+o(|h|).$$ Expanding the smooth function $(K_k)_y$ in its two variables therefore gives $$\{\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}w_c(y_k)+(K_k)_{yy}\}DY_k+(K_k)_{yz}=0.$$ Thus stationary differentiation uses the subgradient expansion even when neighboring endpoints are nonsmooth. Solving it with <a href="#jet:Y-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:Y-identity">[jet:Y-identity]</a> gives, in parallel frames, $$\label{jet:undo-short}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}w_c(y_k)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}G_k(z_k)+O(\tau_k).$$ In particular these endpoint Hessians are uniformly bounded. The error statement follows directly from the displayed short-cost expansions and the bounded inverse in <a href="#jet:Y-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:Y-identity">[jet:Y-identity]</a>; it does not assume a bound for $\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}w_c$ in advance.

<a id="jet:V-sequence"></a>
<a id="jet:center-exact"></a>
*Step 7: insert the true center form and take matrix limits.* Set $$r_k=\tau_k^{-1}\log_{y_k}z_k,\quad
 l_k=\varphi_c'(v(y_k)),\quad
 \widehat x_k=\exp_{y_k}r_k,\quad
 \widehat p_k=\log_{\widehat x_k}y_k.$$ The true center endpoint is $\zeta_k=\exp_{y_k}(r_k/l_k)$, and it is before cut because $y_k\notin N$. Identify $T_{x_k}M$ with $T_{\widehat x_k}M$ by parallel transport over their short joining segment, and let $B_k:T_{x_k}M\to T_{y_k}M$ be the exponential differential at $\widehat p_k$ after that identification. Define $$\label{jet:V-sequence}
 V_k=l_k B_k^*
       \{\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}v(y_k)+A_{y_k}(r_k/l_k)\}B_k>0.$$ The exact chain rule for the scalar modification gives $$\begin{aligned}
 B_k^*\{\mathop{\mathrm{Hess}}w_c(y_k)+A_{y_k}(r_k)\}B_k
 ={}& V_k
 +B_k^*\{A_{y_k}(r_k)-l_kA_{y_k}(r_k/l_k)\}B_k
 \nonumber\\
 &+\frac{\varphi_c''(v(y_k))}{l_k^2}
      \widehat p_k\widehat p_k^* .
 \label{jet:center-exact}
\end{aligned}$$ All vectors on the last line are understood after the same isometric identification. Lemma <a href="#spec:pullback" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:pullback">14</a> bounds the middle term below by $c(1-l_k)\mathrm{Id}$. Equation <a href="#jet:undo-short" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:undo-short">[jet:undo-short]</a> and the vanishing pole mismatch show that the left side differs from $H_k$ by $o(1)\mathrm{Id}$. The verification is the same smooth coordinate comparison as in Lemma <a href="#jet:semibound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:semibound">29</a>, now using the bounded unregularized Hessians in place of the smooth support Hessians. This proves $$H_k\ge V_k+c\frac{b_k}{D}\mathrm{Id}
       -C\frac{b_k}{D^2}p_kp_k^*-o(1)\mathrm{Id}.$$ It also bounds $V_k$ from above, because $V_k>0$ and $H_k$ is bounded.

<a id="jet:V-jacobian"></a>
Taking the determinant in <a href="#jet:V-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:V-sequence">[jet:V-sequence]</a> and using <a href="#jet:ae-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:ae-jacobian">[jet:ae-jacobian]</a> gives the exact factorization $$\label{jet:V-jacobian}
 \det V_k
 =l_k^n\sigma_{\widehat x_k}(\widehat p_k)^2
       \frac{|\det D(\exp_{\cdot}\mathop{\mathrm{grad}}v)(y_k)|}
            {\sigma_{y_k}(r_k/l_k)}
 \ge c\,\frac{\sigma_{\widehat x_k}(\widehat p_k)^2}
                 {\sigma_{y_k}(r_k/l_k)}.$$ The squared factor is the determinant of the Hessian pullback. All scalar derivatives lie between $1/2$ and $2$, so the constants in this density inequality are independent of $K$.

There is no assertion that the limiting endpoint is an Alexandrov point of $v$. We take limits of matrices at the good endpoints just selected. The center modified rays have a uniform extension at the fixed scales: $1-l_k\ge c b_-/D>0$, and the ray through $\widehat x_k$ continues to the true endpoint through duration $1/l_k$. Compactness of this family of proper minimizing subsegments therefore gives $$\sigma_{\widehat x_k}(\widehat p_k)\ge c_{\mathrm{fixed}}>0.$$ Since $0<V_k\le C_{\mathrm{fixed}}\mathrm{Id}$, equation <a href="#jet:V-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:V-jacobian">[jet:V-jacobian]</a> now implies $$\sigma_{y_k}(r_k/l_k)
 \ge \frac{c\,\sigma_{\widehat x_k}(\widehat p_k)^2}
             {\det V_k}
 \ge c'_{\mathrm{fixed}}>0.$$ Continuity of the exponential differential excludes a conjugate limit of the true center rays. An ordinary cut limit may remain; the specified nonsingular branch suffices.

Finally take a subsequence so that $b_k,H_k,L_k,V_k,V_{1,k}$ and all configuration parameters converge, in a smooth orthonormal trivialization. Discard any additional zero limiting weights. The selected weight remains at least $m_*$, and its outward displacement retains the positive fixed-scale lower bound from Step 3. The barycenter identity then gives another surviving vertex, so the surviving difference span is still the same nonzero line. The selected modified and true outer branches were already uniformly nonsingular in Step 4; the center modified branch has its fixed-scale extension. Equation <a href="#jet:V-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:V-jacobian">[jet:V-jacobian]</a> gives a positive lower determinant in the limit, so $V>0$. Equations <a href="#jet:L-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:L-properties">[jet:L-properties]</a>, <a href="#jet:Jensen-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:Jensen-error">[jet:Jensen-error]</a>, and <a href="#jet:center-exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:center-exact">[jet:center-exact]</a> give <a href="#jet:limiting-center" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-center">[jet:limiting-center]</a>.

At each selected outer endpoint in the diagonal sequence, formula <a href="#jet:outer-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:outer-form">[jet:outer-form]</a>, with $H_{o,k}=m_{1,k}L_k$, has a positive divided-action term. Its scalar curvature coefficient satisfies $$-\frac{\varphi_o''(v(y_{1,k}))}{l_{1,k}^2}
 =-\frac{b_kB_o''((v(y_{1,k})-a)/D)}{D^2l_{1,k}^2}
 \ge cK b_k/D^2.$$ Passing this inequality and <a href="#jet:outer-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:outer-det">[jet:outer-det]</a> along $V_{1,k}\to V_i$ gives <a href="#jet:limiting-outer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-outer">[jet:limiting-outer]</a> and the other inequality in <a href="#jet:limiting-determinants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-determinants">[jet:limiting-determinants]</a>. No lower Taylor jet is asserted at the limiting endpoint. The weights, directions, and section-level estimates pass through the same chosen subsequence and give <a href="#jet:limiting-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-geometry">[jet:limiting-geometry]</a>–<a href="#jet:limiting-outward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-outward">[jet:limiting-outward]</a>. Their constants and the cap were fixed before $K$. Only $\delta,M$, the rate of the prefix limit, and the Jensen radii depended on the fixed outer template. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The determinant gain

<a id="stmt-jet-matrix"></a>
<div id="jet:matrix" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (Oblique projection and the missing direction). *Suppose the matrices and vectors satisfy <a href="#jet:limiting-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-geometry">[jet:limiting-geometry]</a>–<a href="#jet:limiting-outer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-outer">[jet:limiting-outer]</a>. Then <a id="jet:matrix-gain"></a>$$\label{jet:matrix-gain}
 \det V_i\ge cK\det V,$$ where $c>0$ depends on the fixed center-template constants, the dimension, and $m_*$, and is independent of $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-matrix"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-matrix</summary>

<a id="jet:projected-matrix"></a>
*Proof.* Put $\beta=Cb/D^2$, increasing $C$ if necessary, and $\gamma=cKb/D^2$. Define $$P=\mathrm{Id}-e p^*/q.$$ This is the projection onto $p^\perp$ along $e$. Since $LE=0$, $P^*LP=L$. On $p^\perp$ the center inequality gives $H\ge V$, and $L\ge H$. Thus $$\label{jet:projected-matrix}
 V_i\ge m_iP^*VP+\gamma p_ip_i^*.$$ Also $H(e,e)\le L(e,e)=0$ implies $V(e,e)\le\beta q^2$.

Let $Q$ have orthonormal columns spanning $p^\perp$, and use the nonsingular basis matrix $[Q,e]$. In this basis write $$[Q,e]^*V[Q,e]
 =\begin{pmatrix} A&z\\z^*&h\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 S=h-z^*A^{-1}z>0.$$ Then $S\le h=V(e,e)\le\beta q^2$. The determinant of the right side of <a href="#jet:projected-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:projected-matrix">[jet:projected-matrix]</a>, in the same basis, is $m_i^{n-1}\det A\,\gamma(p_i\cdot e)^2$. Taking the ratio cancels the determinant of the nonorthogonal change of basis and gives $$\frac{\det V_i}{\det V}
 \ge m_i^{n-1}\frac{\gamma(p_i\cdot e)^2}{S}
 \ge m_i^{n-1}\frac{\gamma}{\beta}
        \frac{(p_i\cdot e)^2}{q^2}.$$ Since $p_i=p+d_ie$ with $d_i>0$ and $q>0$, we have $p_i\cdot e\ge q$. Now use $m_i\ge m_*$ and $\gamma/\beta\ge cK$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-jet-contradiction"></a>
<div id="jet:contradiction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 35** (Contradiction to a non-power oscillation). *The section-oscillation function of Lemma <a href="#sc:plateau" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:plateau">18</a> has a positive power upper bound at zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-jet-contradiction"><summary>Proof of stmt-jet-contradiction</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose otherwise, and take the sequence of scales supplied by that Lemma. Fix all center and weight constants, then choose $K$ and its outer template, and then take sufficiently small scales to apply Proposition <a href="#jet:determinants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:determinants">33</a>. Lemma <a href="#jet:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:matrix">34</a> and <a href="#jet:limiting-determinants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-determinants">[jet:limiting-determinants]</a> imply $$cK\,\frac{\sigma_x(p)^2}{\sigma_y(r/l)}
 \le \det V_i
 \le C\,\frac{\sigma_x(p_i)^2}{\sigma_{y_i}(r_i/l_i)}.$$ The radial comparison of Corollary <a href="#spec:radial-determinants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:radial-determinants">13</a> gives $$\sigma_y(r/l)\le C\sigma_x(p),\qquad
 \sigma_{y_i}(r_i/l_i)\ge c\sigma_x(p_i).$$ For the selected outward endpoint, <a href="#jet:limiting-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-geometry">[jet:limiting-geometry]</a>–<a href="#jet:limiting-outward" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jet:limiting-outward">[jet:limiting-outward]</a> give $m_i\ge m_*$ and $q^2\ge cD$. Its norm increment satisfies $|p_i|^2-|p|^2\le3D$ by Lemma <a href="#sc:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:outward">25</a>. Corollary <a href="#spec:outward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:outward">16</a> therefore applies with $\mu=m_*$, $\kappa=c$, and $L_0=3$, and gives $\sigma_x(p)\ge c'\sigma_x(p_i)$. These parameters were fixed before $K$. Hence $$cK\sigma_x(p)\le C\sigma_x(p_i)\le C'\sigma_x(p).$$ The center modified branch is nonconjugate, so $\sigma_x(p)>0$. This bounds $K$ by a constant fixed before it was chosen. Choosing $K$ larger gives a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Completion of the uniform estimate

Proposition <a href="#jet:contradiction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jet:contradiction">35</a> was applied to the section-oscillation supremum over the entire class of densities with the fixed bounds $\lambda,\Lambda$ on $(M,g)$. It therefore gives constants $C_0<\infty$, $\beta>0$, and $r_0>0$, common to that class, such that $$F(r)\le C_0r^\beta\qquad(0<r<r_0).$$ Lemma <a href="#sc:power" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:power">17</a> now gives a common Hölder estimate with $\alpha=\tfrac12\min\{\beta,1\}$. In particular, every pole has a single contact endpoint. Applying the same estimate to the reversed density pair gives singleton contacts on the other side, with the same constants. The two contact maps are continuous inverses and agree almost everywhere with the optimal maps. Enlarging the Hölder constant to cover distances outside the small-scale range proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 36*. The proof produces a positive exponent by contradiction and compactness. It gives no explicit exponent or estimate uniform over varying metrics or density bounds. The inverse map comes from applying the result to the reversed positive-density pair; no time-one injectivity is asserted for an arbitrary cost potential.

</div>
